# Rubylight 4.1.0 for Windows

Rubylight, called Butterpollo until rc.29, is written in Rust: host, native helpers, service and setup, with a Svelte web console. The 4.1.0 installer is named `rubylight-setup-4.1.0.exe` and upgrades an existing Vibepollo or Butterpollo installation in place, keeping settings, paired devices, the app library and covers. Codec SDKs and Windows drivers remain external components; the setup installs the drivers.

## New in 4.1.0

- **Frame Warp follows a turn all over the picture.** In a 3D game a camera turn moves the edges of the picture further than its middle. The host now learns the game's field of view from your turns and Nightjar turns the picture the way the camera does. Measured on the RX 7900 XT with a test game at 90 degrees: Frame Warp removes 96.0% of a turn's displacement at the centre (4.0.0: 84.2%), 91.8% over the whole picture (73.6%) and 90.8% at its edge (65.4%); at 70 degrees 93.0% at the edge, at 110 degrees 86.3%. From about 7 seconds of turning on, the learned field of view stayed within 3.5 degrees of the game's (86.6 to 88.9 for 90, 67.8 to 69.3 for 70, 107.6 for 110). Needs Nightjar 5.1.0.
- The motion measurement leaves out everything that stays put on the screen while the picture moves (minimaps, bars, a HUD's rim), not only the HUD the client holds still. Such pieces held the learned turn back near the picture's corners.
- Nightjar 5.0 keeps sliding the picture and now gets the turn of the picture's centre: 96.2% removed at the centre in the same test (4.0.0: 84.2%), 58.5% at the edge (65.4%).
- A turn left or right while the camera looks far up or down also rolls the picture, which Frame Warp does not follow: at about 7 degrees of pitch that is most of what is left at the picture's edge.
- Games that move alike everywhere (maps, side-scrollers) get no lens and behave as before: 96.4% removed in the same test.
- **Setup's refusal no longer names Rubylight 3.0.** On a PC it cannot run on, setup says "This PC cannot run Rubylight"; the troubleshooting heading and Log analysis's link to it dropped the version too.
- **Keep virtual speakers while paused.** A new setting under Audio, Virtual speakers, off by default: when a device disconnects and its game keeps running, the virtual speakers stay the Windows default device, so the PC's own speakers stay silent. Your own device comes back when the game stops or with End session, and a device that reconnects takes the held route up again (issue #93).
- **PyroWave no longer drops out after some host starts.** On some PCs, the check that decides whether PyroWave is offered failed on about half of the host starts, and PyroWave then stayed off until the next restart. The check now ends without closing the encoders it tested, so a fault while closing them can no longer fail it, and a failed check runs up to two more times. When it still fails, the log shows how the check ended (issue #94).
- **Searching the log no longer freezes the page.** A short search marked every match in all 5000 lines at once, tens of thousands of them, on every typed character. The console now marks the matches around the lines in view; the count and Next/Previous still cover the whole log. Measured in Chrome with 5000 lines: one typed character held the page for 1.5 s before and 0.2 s now.

**Tested.** Frame Warp was measured in loopback on the RX 7900 XT at 2560x1440, H.264 at 60 fps, 60 seconds a run, with a test game drawn through a camera of 70, 90 and 110 degrees and nightjar-cli logging the shift of every picture; [Performance](PERFORMANCE.md#november-12-frame-warp-lens) has the runs. The host's motion measurement takes 1.6 ms for each compared picture on its own thread (0.5 ms in 4.0.2), off the frame's path. The lens has not been run on a real game or judged on a client's screen, and Nightjar's lens shaders for Android, macOS and Linux were compiled and unit-tested, not watched on a device. NVIDIA was not run. The log search was timed in Chrome with 5000 lines.

## New in 4.0.2

- **Game settings per device go back as soon as the game closes.** A game that had exited could still count as running while another program, such as Steam or Playnite, held on to it. Its settings files then stayed in place, the console warned that they were not back yet, and starting the game again was refused until they were (issue #82).
- **Keep monitors off while paused now lasts as long as the display is kept.** With an exclusive layout, the monitors came back on a few seconds after a device disconnected, whatever **Keep a disconnected display for** said (issue #92). With **Keep monitors off while paused** on and **Restore displays on disconnect** off, they now stay off for that time, or until the app closes. The restore hotkey still brings them back at any time.
- **Steam no longer offers a firmware update for the emulated Steam Controller.** With the Steam Controller pad type on a device that has no real Steam Controller, Steam saw older firmware than it ships, offered an update that cannot reach a virtual controller, and kept the notice after it failed (issue #90). The virtual controller now reports firmware Steam does not offer to update. A device with a real Steam Controller still reports that controller's own firmware.

**Tested.** A unit test ends a process while its handle stays open and checks that the host no longer counts it as running. The restore delays are covered for each combination of the paused-display settings. The Steam Controller's firmware request is checked byte for byte against the driver's layout. Steam's firmware prompt and the reporters' monitors were not reproduced here.

## New in 4.0.1

- **A stream Windows refused the display layout to now tries again by itself.** The first stream after the PC sat idle could fail with "cannot activate the saved displays … Access is denied" while the next one started (issue #89). The host now waits two seconds and prepares the display once more, and the log names the desktop that had input at that moment.
- **Firefox: page changes in the console no longer shrink and jump back.** Switching pages fades the window behind the sidebar, which Firefox draws at full size; before, it drew the page as a cut-off slice that shrank and then snapped back.
- **Settings that run on NVIDIA no longer say AMD.** Copy and convert on a compute queue and GPU fence transport describe what they do on Radeon and NVIDIA graphics cards, and the PyroWave and couch-menu messages no longer name AMD or Radeon.

**Tested.** In Firefox 160 the page change's before and after pictures are now both the window's size (1440x814, the sidebar 247x814) where they were 915 and 836 px slices of a 1328 px page; Chrome still crossfades with the sidebar in place. The retry is covered by a unit test of the refusal it recognises; the idle state that made Windows refuse the layout on the reporter's PC was not reproduced here.

## New in 4.0.0

Rubylight 4.0.0 is a major release for Radeon and NVIDIA graphics. It covers everything since 3.0.0, from 3.0.1 to 3.11.10 and the changes since. It brings Frame Warp, which shows a camera turn on the next refresh of a Nightjar device, a console built around one settings layout, NVIDIA graphics cards with native YUV 4:4:4 and their driver settings, and new ways to play together: guests in the browser, a second screen on one device, and a running game that moves between devices. Moonlight clients keep working, and features that need the client's support go to clients that ask for them. Nightjar 5.0 is the matching client.

### Highlights

- **Frame Warp: camera turns on the next refresh.** With Frame Warp on in Nightjar, a mouse or right-stick turn shows on the device's next screen refresh, and the game's HUD stays in place. Rubylight learns each game while you play. In our test it removed 96% of the picture's distance from where the mouse said it should be.
- **A console that leads with what matters.** Home opens on pairing for a new PC, lists what needs attention with a button that fixes it, and shows the game you play. All settings sits in six tabs, and Ctrl+K finds any game, device, setting or action.
- **Built for Radeon and NVIDIA.** NVIDIA graphics cards stream with NVENC again: H.264, HEVC and AV1, HDR, native YUV 4:4:4, and NVIDIA's driver settings from the console and Nightjar.
- **Play together in more ways.** Friends watch or play along in their browser, a device with two screens shows a second PC screen, and a running game moves to another device in one press or plays on both.
- **Play from a browser.** A TV, console or car browser pairs once and plays with no app, and Firefox streams too.
- **Every launcher works like Steam.** Epic Games, Battle.net, Microsoft Store and Xbox, EA app, Ubisoft Connect, GOG and your game folders each sync on their own terms, and each device can keep its own game settings.
- **The PC from the couch.** A paired device approves a new one, restarts, shuts down or updates the PC, and serves as the PC's microphone with no stream.
- **Frames leave sooner.** 4K frames go out slice by slice, large frames start leaving before the whole frame is ready, and streams stay timed to the device's refresh after the PC was busy.

### Picture and encoding

- **Frame Warp: camera turns on the next refresh.** For a Nightjar client that turns Frame Warp on, Rubylight learns each game while you play (how far the picture moves per mouse count and per second of right stick, how long the game takes to show an input, and where its HUD is) and tags every frame with the input it shows, so Nightjar shifts each picture by the turn the game has not drawn yet and leaves the HUD in place. What a game taught it is kept for its next session. All settings › Picture › Frame pacing › Let devices use Frame Warp (`frame_warp`, on by default) lets clients use it; nothing runs until a client asks. In our test it removed 96% of the picture's distance from where the mouse said it should be, and its GPU passes take 0.14 to 0.16 ms a frame (median) from 1080p to 4K.
- **4K streams arrive sooner.** H.264 and HEVC streams with 4K-size pictures (3840x2160, 4096x2160, 5120x1440) send each frame slice by slice while the encoder finishes the rest, with no client change. Smaller pictures still go out whole, because there it measured no gain. `video_slice_delivery = off` turns it off, and `2` or `4` turn it on for every size.
- **Large frames start leaving sooner.** An idle sender gets each error-correction block of an H.264, HEVC or AV1 frame as soon as it is built instead of waiting for the whole frame, and PyroWave frames go out that way too. Frames that fit one block don't change. `video_send_early_blocks` (on by default) turns it off.
- **Keyframes leave sooner.** H.264 keyframes no longer have the whole picture scanned one byte at a time to adjust their header, and HEVC pictures whose header Rubylight rewrites reuse the host's pooled frame buffers.
- **Streams stay timed to your screen after the PC was busy.** With Nightjar, the host times frames to arrive just before the device's screen refreshes (phase lock). After a build or a loading game, the phase lock could lose that refresh until the stream restarted. The host now notices and finds it again within a few seconds.
- **Games running faster than the stream reach the screen in time.** A game at 120 fps streamed at 60 fps no longer ends up with most frames just missing the refresh and waiting a whole extra one.
- **Games capped at the stream's frame rate no longer wait on the host.** A game held to the stream rate by RTSS or its own limiter now has its frames go out as they arrive; the log says "phase lock stands aside" when this happens. All settings › Picture › Frame pacing › Hold capped games for the device's refresh brings the hold back.
- **Steadier fixed-rate streams beside other windows.** When a game runs at two to four times the stream rate, the host follows the game's own frame timing, so another window's updates no longer take the place of the game's frames.
- **Fixed-rate pacing no longer claims a frame up to 1 ms late.** With `frame_pacing = grid` the host stopped waiting in an encoder output query close to its tick.
- **Frame timing keeps working after a refresh change.** On Windows Graphics Capture, a game or Windows changing the display's refresh during a stream no longer leaves the host timing frames against the old refresh.
- **The Radeon encoder runs at the driver's bitrate ceiling.** A device that asks for more than the AMD driver's encoder accepts at that size and frame rate no longer gets "AMF error AMF_OUT_OF_RANGE". The encoder runs at the driver's own ceiling, and the stream's warnings and the host log say which rate it runs at. This applies to H.264, HEVC and AV1, and to bitrate changes during a stream.
- **Full chroma on HEVC at 2560x1600 and other large sizes.** The check before a full-chroma stream counted each of a large frame's four slices as a frame and refused the stream with "packed encoder failed to return frame 2". It now counts whole frames.
- **Full chroma survives a start with Windows.** When the host started before anyone signed in, its 4:4:4 check failed until the host restarted. The check now waits for the sign-in.
- **Long-term references per device.** Devices › Encoder overrides lets one device turn long-term reference frames off, for a decoder that freezes on them, while the others keep the host's setting.
- **Steadier HDR metadata when a picture keeps jumping.** HDR10+, HDR Vivid and Dolby Vision metadata still snaps to a new scene at once, but further jumps within a second glide over about 15 samples, so the display's tone mapping no longer moves several times a second.
- **PyroWave does less work per frame.** Large frames reuse their buffers instead of allocating megabytes per frame, pictures go from the encoder's output straight into network packets, and D3D11 full-chroma streams can convert 8- and 10-bit pictures on the GPU's compute queue.
- **Overlap PyroWave frames.** All settings › Picture › Codecs › Overlap PyroWave frames prepares the next PyroWave frame while the GPU still encodes the current one. It is off by default: at 4K and 120 fps it measured no lower latency.
- **AMF output options, both off.** `amd_output_prediction` skips output queries the oldest frame can't answer yet, and `amd_output_thread` collects AMF output on a thread of its own. Under GPU load prediction gave no consistent gain; the thread cut picture age p99 by 2.5 to 9 ms but made the typical frame 0.8 to 1.7 ms later at 1440p120 and used more CPU.
- **Less work per frame and per packet.** The host keeps colour conversion views between frames, waits instead of spinning while the Radeon encoder's input is full, reuses buffers for HDR metadata and packets, watches the presents of the processes it streams, and looks a frame's present time up directly. The network and display checks run on their own threads instead of in the frame path.
- AMD encoding builds against AMF 1.5.3, which corrects how the host queries the driver's bitrate and long-term reference limits.

### Network and bitrate

- **Automatic error correction keeps a floor.** Nightjar's Automatic error correction keeps at least 10 % recovery data. Before, it dropped to none after one clean second, and on real Wi-Fi any lost packet then cost a frame and a keyframe. The PC's setting stays the default.
- **PyroWave on constrained links.** The host lowers the video bitrate when frames are lost while sending, alongside the losses the client reports. It also bounds bursts on routes without client bandwidth hints and paces recovery after congestion.
- **Faster error correction on newer processors.** On CPUs with GFNI (AMD Zen 4 and newer, Intel Ice Lake and newer) the host computes error correction with it, with the same bytes. `fec_gfni` (on by default).
- **Error correction finishes each packet in larger steps.** The last bytes of each packet go 16 and 8 bytes at a time instead of one by one.
- **Room for very high bitrates.** Above about 840 Mbps of pacing, the host's video socket buffer grows with the pace to hold 10 ms of traffic, up to 8 MB, instead of a fixed 1 MB. Lower bitrates keep the 1 MB buffer.
- **`pacing_follow_client_link`** (off by default) lifts the 800 Mbps ceiling of H.264, HEVC and AV1 on a wired route when the device reports its own link: the ceiling becomes 80 % of the slower of the two links.
- **WebRTC streams hand each frame to the network 2.2-2.7x faster, with 5-12% less host CPU.** Rubylight writes each run of equal-sized packets to Windows in one call instead of one call per packet. This covers browsers and Samsung TVs.
- IPv4 clients with `address_family = both` now count IPv4 headers in every send budget and batch size.

### Audio

- **The streamed microphone sounds like itself.** Steam Streaming Microphone passes what the host plays straight to its recording side, and with its default formats a voice reached games and chat apps pitched down to less than half and chopped up. The host now gives the playing side the recording side's format before it plays. If the format can't be set, the session shows a warning.
- **The streamed microphone no longer clips into noise.** While a client's microphone plays, Rubylight keeps Windows' recording slider for Steam Streaming Microphone at 0 dB or lower, so the level set in Nightjar is the level apps hear. A lower level stays as you set it.
- **A late microphone packet no longer breaks the voice off.** When packets arrive in bursts, the gap is filled with the Opus decoder's concealment, faded out past 40 ms and crossfaded back into the voice. The delay stays the same.
- **Microphones that send in bursts no longer drop in chunks.** The host learns how far apart packets keep arriving and holds up to twice that (at most 150 ms). A steady device keeps the delay it had.
- **The PC's microphone, with no stream.** A Nightjar device can be the PC's microphone with no stream. It needs Launch apps, takes no input, starts no app and leaves the PC's sound on the PC.
- **Sound comes back after a late audio ping.** A stream whose client's audio ping missed the opening 10 seconds stayed silent until you reconnected. The host now keeps waiting, shows a warning while the ping is missing, and sends sound as soon as it arrives.
- AC3 and E-AC3 audio passthrough uses FFmpeg 8.1.3.
- The host log names Steam Streaming Microphone's playback and recording formats each time a client's microphone starts, and `microphone stopped` counts underruns and concealment.

### Play together

- **Share your browser stream with friends.** While you stream on the console's Stream page, **Guests → Invite** makes a link for up to four guests: view, controller, or controller with keyboard and mouse, lasting 1 hour, 24 hours or a time you pick (5 minutes to 7 days). Guests open it in their browser, type a name and see and hear your stream; no app, account or pairing. The list shows who watches, with Kick, and every link, with Revoke.
- **One encode for every guest.** The PC encodes once and each guest gets the same frames over its own connection, so a guest on a slow link drops frames alone. With UPnP and the WebRTC server on, the PC asks the router to forward the guest page and one media port per guest, and removes them when the host stops.
- **Guests are safe by default.** The link's token travels after `#` and the host keeps just its hash, wrong links are rate-limited per address, a guest's page reaches nothing of the console, and the host checks every guest input against the invite's permission. Guests see a certificate warning, so keep keyboard-and-mouse links for your own network or VPN.
- **Dual screen.** Nightjar's Dual screen mode streams a second display to the same device next to the stream it already has, under the pairing it already has. Each screen keeps its own resolution, scale, bitrate changes, clipboard and statistics, and the host puts the two virtual displays back where you left them the last time. With a game running, the second screen gets a virtual display of its own beside the game's; on a PC that streams its own monitors, it streams the PC's other monitor.
- **Continue here.** A paired device with View and Launch takes the running game's stream from another device in one press: the other device's stream ends, the game keeps running and that device is told where its stream went. A press that fails gives the game back to the device it came from, still running.
- **Stream here too.** A second device shares the display the game is on, scaled to the device, while the game's resolution, refresh, HDR and scale stay as they were.
- **Background streams start the game, once.** Rubylight's Nucleus Co-op profile now says "any processor", closes the box that a click made too early brings up, and presses Play itself, once, after the profile is loaded. Games get pads by slot, so no stream stalls on "Press a button on Player1's gamepad". A start that times out says where Nucleus Co-op stopped.
- **Your controller reaches the background game.** A background or co-op stream unplugs the controllers earlier sessions left plugged in, so the game reads yours. A game that refuses Nucleus Co-op's input hooks gets them in another way, takes the controller from the start, and gets its hooks back after false refusals.
- **The game stays off your monitors, keyboard and mouse.** Unreal Engine games start in a window at the stream's resolution, a game window loses its title bar, a game that keeps coming back to a monitor is put back for the whole stream, and the PC's keyboard and mouse stay out of the game, DirectInput games included. One click elsewhere on the PC frees the mouse from a game that refused the hooks.
- **The game's sound stays off the PC.** In background mode, with the stream carrying the game's sound alone, the game plays on a virtual playback device nobody hears while the client still gets it, moved once the game opens its sound, and goes back to its own device when the game closes. A device's "play audio on the PC" no longer brings a background stream's game sound to the PC.
- **The PC says when a device starts a background or co-op stream.** A Windows notification names the device and the game, once per stream.
- **A game that replaces its window as the stream starts keeps the stream.** The capture gives up on a closed or hidden window at once and opens the new one, and a capture that waited the whole five seconds gets another try when the game has opened a new window.
- **Epic games in background streams.** A game the Epic Games Launcher installed starts with the arguments that say which game it is, without its sign-in, so it shouldn't restart through the launcher on the PC's monitor.
- **Quitting a background game ends it**, also after the stream itself already ended, and a background stream whose game window doesn't come back ends with the reason after 90 seconds.
- **One stuck file no longer blocks every background stream.** Rubylight checks your copy of Nucleus Co-op before each start and makes a fresh one when a file is stuck. Game and Nucleus Co-op settings an earlier session changed always come back.
- A Nucleus Co-op stream of a game that waits on a "Mandatory ASLR" box ends with the reason and says how to turn the Windows setting off for that game.
- A Moonlight stream that joins another device's game from the Resume or Running Game tile no longer ends that game.

### Games and library

- **Every launcher works like Steam.** Epic Games, Battle.net, Microsoft Store and Xbox, EA app, Ubisoft Connect, GOG and your game folders each have their own options under All settings › Games: sync automatically, remove games you uninstall, and games to leave out. Battle.net can also keep just the games you played recently, as Steam can. Your earlier shared choices carry over to every launcher.
- **Sync now always works.** Turning off a launcher's automatic sync stops the background check alone; Sync now still adds its games, as with Steam.
- **Epic box art.** Epic games get their box art from the Epic Games launcher's own files. A cover you chose is never replaced.
- **Clearer game pages.** A synced game's page says which launcher starts it, saving it confirms with a short message instead of a banner, and a launcher that isn't installed shows a switch you can't turn on.
- **Remove several games at once, and bring them back.** Select on the Games page puts a checkbox on every cover; removed games stay out of your library, and Restore removed games brings back the ones still installed.
- **Game settings per device.** List a game's own settings files in its app, and each device keeps its own copy: the TV its 4K settings, the handheld its 1080p ones. The PC's own files come back when the game closes, and the last three are kept as backups.
- **Collections without setup.** Each library entry names its Steam collections and Playnite categories, so Nightjar can group Home by them. Steam's files are read, never written.
- **Steam installs show up sooner.** With auto sync on, an install or removal reaches Nightjar in about a minute instead of up to 5 minutes. Library scans run at background priority, Steam's app list is read again after Steam changed it, and a game the store has no cover for is asked about less often.
- **One exit timeout for every app.** All settings › Games › App behavior › Exit timeout sets how long the host waits for an app to close before ending it, 10 seconds unless changed. An app's own exit timeout still wins.
- **Wait after a game closes.** All settings › Games › Playnite › Wait after a game closes sets how long a Playnite game must stay closed before its stream ends, 15 seconds unless changed. A game started through Playnite no longer ends with a false "game not detected" warning.
- The library tells clients when each game was last played, from Steam and Playnite, and a game without a cover of its own has none, so Nightjar shows its title.
- Xbox PC games that start through `GameLaunchHelper.exe`, and configs saved as UTF-16, show up in Add games.
- Pause and resume commands start without waiting, so a script that keeps running while the game is paused no longer holds back the resume.
- Listing the running programs takes a third of the time: the host reads the process list in one call and opens a program once.

### Displays

- **An extended stream no longer changes your monitor after an exclusive one.** The end of an exclusive stream no longer saves a Windows layout that puts your monitor at 60 Hz with the virtual display as main. A stale entry is put back when it shows up.
- **A stream going on keeps its layout when another display leaves.** Monitors that were off stay off, and a game the leaving display minimised comes back.
- **Monitors no longer stay dark after a shared session.** A layout is saved while a virtual display it was made for is still there, and never under your own monitors' entry.
- **A sleeping monitor no longer leaves a TV on alone when an exclusive stream ends.** The virtual display stays in the background until the monitor is back, then your layout is put back. Nothing waits for that, and a new stream starts right away.
- **A monitor that keeps reconnecting no longer blanks the stream.** After the same monitor comes back three times within five minutes, the host leaves it on for the rest of the stream and says so on the stream card.
- **A browser on the PC itself keeps its monitors on.** The `exclusive` layout, and `ensure_only_display` on a physical display, run as `extended_primary` for such a stream.
- A resolution switch the encoder refuses puts the display, the co-op window and the bitrate back to the mode the stream keeps, and a second stream that joins a device's display leaves that display with the device it belongs to.
- The permanent virtual display count (`dd_virtual_display_permanent_count`), which nothing has read since permanent virtual displays were removed, is dropped from saved settings.

### Devices and input

- **Play from a TV, console or car browser, with no app.** With the WebRTC server on, open `https://<PC address>:47991/play` on the local network or Tailscale, pair once with the PIN the page shows, then pick a game with the remote, the arrow keys or a controller and play full screen. Hold Back or Select for a second for Disconnect and Quit game.
- **Stream in the browser picks the codec, resolution and frame rate.** The Stream page offers AV1, HEVC and H.264 at every resolution and frame rate the host's encoder takes, limited to what the browser can receive, and remembers your choice.
- **Firefox streams in the browser**, also on the PC itself at `127.0.0.1` or `localhost`, and opens invite links and consoles from another Rubylight PC.
- **Browser streams say why they can't connect.** The page says whether the browser never tried, the PC didn't answer at this address and UDP port, or the encrypted connection failed, each with its fix.
- **No green rows under AV1 in the browser.** The Play, Stream and guest pages leave out the rows the AMD encoder pads 1080p AV1 with, and the pointer maps to the picture without them.
- **Approve a new device from a paired one.** A device with the new **Approve new devices** permission sees who waits to pair and enters the PIN the new device shows. The new device gets Play, never more than the approver holds, and every approval raises a toast, a log line and the `pairing.paired` webhook.
- **Restart and shut down the PC from Nightjar.** A new per-device permission, "Restart and shut down PC", off by default, lets a device restart or shut down the PC while nothing runs on it. Each request needs a fresh confirmation, and apps with unsaved work are never forced closed.
- **Update the host from a client.** A device with Host control can install the host's pending update. It is refused while another device streams, naming it, and never ends a stream another device starts afterwards.
- **Steam Controller as a controller type.** Games and Steam see a wired 2026 Steam Controller with both trackpads, back grips, grip and stick touch, motion and a battery. A client with a real Steam Controller that announces it also gets Steam's pad haptics and settings.
- **Xbox 360 (ViGEmBus) controller type** (`gamepad = vigem_x360`), for remapping tools that look for USB controllers. It needs ViGEmBus installed separately; without it the stream says to install it.
- **A controller type per device.** Devices → device → Controller sets the virtual controller for that device alone.
- **Updated virtual gamepad driver (0.1.0.92).** A virtual DualSense no longer shows up twice in Steam, the Switch Pro's gyro turns at the right speed with rumble and battery levels, DualShock 4 and DualSense motion timing follows the driver's clock, two fingers no longer merge into one touch, and pads survive sleep and resume more reliably.
- **DualSense vibration stops when Steam stops it**, with waveform haptics (usbip-win2 installed).
- **Xbox 360 (ViGEmBus) rumble arrives at once** and stops when the game stops it.
- **Rumble no longer piles up.** Each poll takes every report a virtual pad has waiting and forwards the newest. A DualSense or DualShock 4 keeps its lightbar and trigger effects.
- **A pinch is one touch frame.** All moving fingers of a pass reach Windows together, in one injection.
- **Input comes before housekeeping.** USB DualSense, Steam Deck and browser input threads run at input priority, and the control thread no longer waits on display lookups.
- **`gamepad_feedback_fast_poll`** (off by default) polls the pads every 2 ms for 250 ms after a change, at about twice the gamepad thread's CPU. **`gamepad_driver_host_priority`** (off by default) runs the process that hosts the gamepad driver at high priority while a VHF pad is plugged in. **`absolute_left_release_delay`** (milliseconds, 10 by default) sets how long a left release waits after absolute mouse, touch or pen input, so a long press can right-click.
- **Wake-on-LAN with Hyper-V external switches.** Physical network adapters whose IP bindings belong to a virtual switch now count when the host chooses the wake address it sends to Moonlight and Nightjar.
- Known issue: with the DualSense from the virtual gamepad driver (without usbip-win2), Steam's rumble stop can still be lost. Install usbip-win2 or choose DualShock 4 emulation.

### Console and setup

- **Home leads with what matters.** On a new PC it opens on a QR code and a PIN to pair a device. Once a device is paired it shows the game that is running or was played last, and while you stream, the stream's live frame rate and bitrate.
- **Problems come with their fix.** Home lists what needs attention, most important at the top, and its button does the fix: turn on waking this PC from a device, switch between a virtual display and a monitor, or allow pairing. Everything that is fine folds into one line.
- **Pairing in one step.** The QR code and the PIN sit side by side, the choice of what a device may do waits until a device is paired, and with pairing off the QR code stays in place behind one Allow pairing button. When a device pairs, Home greets it by name.
- **Settings you can find.** All settings has six tabs (This PC, Picture, Sound & controls, Games, Network, Advanced), and a section bar follows you down long pages. The Settings home keeps the few things most people change, with waking this PC as one switch. Intervals, timeouts, sizes and resolutions are menus with a Custom option instead of raw seconds and milliseconds. Every setting keeps its key, and old links still open the right place.
- **Help that helps.** Help holds connection help, what the log says and the support bundle. Restarting Rubylight, display recovery, the Windows session, Nucleus Co-op's status and crash reports moved to Advanced › Troubleshoot.
- **Search everything with Ctrl+K.** Games, devices, settings and actions such as Pair a device, Sync games now or Restart Rubylight, from any page.
- **Calmer and smoother.** One style for every fold, an (i) beside a setting for its finer points, and pages that fade into each other while a game's cover grows into its page, unless the system asks for reduced motion.
- **The console says when an encoder setting slows the stream.** An AMD setting changed to a value that adds delay or weakens recovery says what it costs under its menu, and Settings lists every such setting you changed. The numbers are our RX 7900 XT measurements.
- **Pairing from the tray and the phone.** The pairing toast opens the Devices page with the PIN field focused, the tray has a **Pair a device** item, and **Open in Nightjar** next to **Copy pairing link** pairs a console opened on a phone in one click.
- **Clients learn why a stream failed.** A stream that ends on an encoder error tells Moonlight it was the encoder, and Nightjar shows the host's own words. A stream larger than the encoder takes, or an encoder setting that doesn't fit the PC, ends with the limit or the fix.
- **The host's warnings on Home.** Settings replaced after a move from another host, missing codecs and a Windows Firewall rule that blocks Rubylight show on Home with Dismiss.
- **The Wake-on-LAN check reads the adapter again**, and offers the fix when the adapter's wake settings are off.
- Turning on browser streaming is one button on the Stream page.

### NVIDIA

- **NVIDIA graphics cards stream again.** Rubylight encodes with NVENC on NVIDIA graphics cards: H.264, HEVC, and AV1 where the card's encoder offers it, with HDR, HDR10+, HDR Vivid and Dolby Vision. With the encoder set to Automatic, the host picks NVENC or AMF from the graphics card that captures. Intel graphics stay on Rubylight 2.6.x.
- **Native YUV 4:4:4 on NVIDIA GPUs.** A device with its YUV 4:4:4 option on gets full color detail in H.264 (8-bit) and HEVC (8-bit and 10-bit, SDR and HDR) from an NVIDIA GPU whose video encoder reports 4:4:4 support. At startup the host encodes a test picture in each 4:4:4 mode and offers the ones that work, and the console's host readiness lists them. AV1 4:4:4 is not available on NVIDIA; choose HEVC for 4:4:4. A device that asks for a mode the host did not offer gets an error naming the reason. AMD GPUs keep 4:4:4 through PyroWave or Full chroma.
- **NVIDIA driver settings from the console and Nightjar.** On a host that streams with an NVIDIA GPU, the graphics settings panel (Nightjar's stream menu and the game page in the console) controls NVIDIA's Low Latency Mode, Max frame rate, Vertical sync, Power management mode, Texture filtering quality, Anisotropic filtering and Smooth Motion (driver 571.86 or newer). As with Radeon settings, the host records the PC's own values before it changes them and puts them back when the session ends, after a crash too. A value the panel can't put back exactly, such as one set with a profile editor, is shown and left alone.
- **Game presets work on NVIDIA.** Competitive sets Low Latency Mode to Ultra, Smoothness caps the frame rate at the stream's, and Quality sets 16x anisotropic filtering with high-quality texture filtering. On Radeon the presets set what they did before.
- **NVENC peak bitrate.** All settings › Picture › NVIDIA NVENC › Peak bitrate sets how far variable bitrate may go above the stream bitrate, from 1× to 3×. 1.5× stays the default.
- **NVIDIA Reflex is back as an RTSS limit mode.** All settings › Picture › RTSS › RTSS limit mode offers NVIDIA Reflex again, and a host that streams with an NVIDIA GPU uses it on its own for virtual-display streams and frame-generation games. On other GPUs a chosen Reflex mode limits with front edge sync and says so in the log. A setting saved as Reflex is no longer changed to Asynchronous at startup.
- **Full GPU clocks while streaming.** The host sets NVIDIA's power management to "Prefer maximum performance" for itself, in a driver profile named Rubylight that covers the host alone. An idle host uses no GPU. All settings › Picture › NVIDIA NVENC › Full GPU clocks while streaming (`nvenc_latency_over_power`) keeps the driver's own power management.
- **Loading screens no longer restart the encoder.** NVENC waits as AMF does: the stream restarts a silent encoder after 250 ms, and waits up to 2 s if it stays silent.
- **Bitrate changes apply in place.** On drivers that change the bitrate in place, a higher bitrate from adaptive bitrate or error correction applies at once instead of waiting for the frames in flight and sending a keyframe.
- **A clear message when the encoder sessions are taken.** When another app holds the GPU's encoder sessions, the stream says so, and the startup check no longer opens a second session.
- **Realtime GPU priority.** On NVIDIA the host runs at realtime GPU priority with hardware-accelerated GPU scheduling on, unless you turn off Realtime GPU priority with hardware scheduling (`nvenc_realtime_hags`).
- NVENC encodes from the compute-queue colour conversion on a D3D12 session (driver 522.25 or newer), keeps frames within the same frame cap as AMF, encodes an oversize scene cut again as a keyframe, and with slice delivery on sends a large picture's slices as they finish. AV1 starts on drivers from 522.25.
- NVIDIA streams wake as NVENC finishes a frame (`nvenc_output_event`, on by default), and a recovery frame after packet loss waits on the encoder's completion signal.
- Texts on the console, in Help and in warnings fit both kinds of GPU, so an NVIDIA PC no longer reads about AMD drivers.

### For client authors

- **Frame Warp control messages.** Control messages `0x5543` (request, client to host), `0x5544` (model), `0x5545` (tags) and `0x5546` (HUD mask), offered as `FrameWarpVersion` in serverinfo while `frame_warp` is on. [Control messages](../docs/control-messages.md), [Frame Warp](../docs/frame-warp.md).
- **Moving and sharing a game.** `POST /session/continue-here` takes the running game's stream from another device; `GET /session` names the device playing the running game; `/resume` lets a device join beside the owner and answers 409 when that would end another stream or names another game.
- **Power and updates.** `GET /power`, `POST /power/restart`, `POST /power/shutdown`, `GET /update` and `POST /update/install`, with the webhooks `power.restart`, `power.shutdown` and `update.started`. [Native API](../docs/native-api.md).
- **Microphone sessions** without a stream are listed as `session.mic_only` in the native API.
- **Library data.** Each library entry names its Steam collections and Playnite categories and when the game was last played.
- **Input timings in the host log.** The 5 s `stream timings` line adds `input_receive_to_injected_ms` and `input_passes`, and a `gamepad timings` line every 5 s shows queue times, queue depth and feedback polls.

### Install, updates and service

- **Choose the time of day Rubylight looks for updates.** All settings › This PC › Updates › Update check time, for example 03:00. With Install updates automatically on, a new release installs after that check once nothing is streaming.
- **Updates say whose stream they wait for.** Updates names the device whose stream an Install now waits for and still offers to install without waiting.
- **Try Rubylight beside the host you have.** When setup finds another streaming host, it asks: replace it, which imports its settings and uninstalls it, or keep it and install alongside (`--side-by-side` for a quiet install). Alongside, Rubylight gets its own service (`RubylightService`), its own ports (base 48589, or the next free range) and new settings, so devices pair again.
- **Setup no longer unpacks programs into ProgramData.** Setup, updates and in-app downloads use a `.staging` folder inside the Rubylight folder, which administrators alone can change, and remove it when they finish.
- **The service file stays the same while the service version is unchanged**, so antivirus programs see a file they already know after an update. Every Rubylight executable names its company, copyright and original file name in its file properties.
- **Fewer hidden PowerShell runs.** The Wake-on-LAN check and fix, setup's network adapter list and driver removal ask Windows directly instead of starting PowerShell in the background.
- **Rubylight no longer downloads Nucleus Co-op or reserves memory inside it.** The game page and Help link to the Nucleus Co-op 2.4.2 release, and Rubylight checks the folder you choose before it keeps it. To find Nucleus Co-op's Play button, Rubylight asks Windows' accessibility interface instead.
- **Controllers work again where the virtual gamepad device was removed.** Setup and updates set the device up again when its driver is left in Windows without it.
- **USB audio and haptics installs when Windows' PATH variable has a malformed entry.** A launch whose controllers did not appear names the missing driver.
- **Each host's certificate root has a name of its own**, so Firefox on a PC with Rubylight shows the usual warning for another host's pages instead of refusing them.
- **Setup finishes an interrupted permission repair**, moves a leftover `C:\ProgramData\Butterpollo` that your own account owns aside instead of stopping, and leaves your own copy of Nucleus Co-op alone on a reinstall.
- If Rubylight 3.0 to 3.2.1 changed your encoder setting from NVENC to AMD AMF, set Encoder back to Automatic to stream from an NVIDIA card, and set the NVENC settings again.
- Setup elevating with a folder that ends in a backslash keeps the arguments after it, and removal deletes Rubylight's service when the registered path differs in case.

### Fixes

- A stream above 250 fps, a sound device with rear height speakers alone, a Playnite date with unusual characters, a deeply nested Steam file or a hand-written Lossless Scaling start delay no longer stop the host.
- Ending a game's leftover processes can no longer end an unrelated program that took over one of their process ids.
- A device that may view streams and nothing more can no longer start a Remote Monitor or Remote Input session.
- The text a host text field sends to the client's keyboard keeps its last character whole.
- A virtual controller the driver refuses to unplug keeps its slot until the session ends.
- The frame limiter no longer stays stuck after a stream when RTSS reports the restored cap in another form.
- A stream to a virtual display no longer logs a frame limiter warning when the limiter is off on purpose.
- A game's "Let Lossless Scaling start by itself" can follow the host setting, and an untouched game no longer turns it off.
- The library marks the game that runs, not every game with its name.
- Neural Rendering names what failed (install, removal or the check at launch) and keeps refreshing after it is switched.
- The console keeps the covers and icons it already loaded and asks the host whether they changed.
- The Games page no longer breaks when a launcher scan answers without a game list.
- The missing Windows HDR Calibration warning shows once per host start instead of at every stream.
- Full chroma's description no longer says "on AMD".
- With no stream, launch or game running, the host's housekeeping wakes when there is something to do instead of checking constantly.

**Tested.** On the RX 7900 XT PC with Windows 11 (build 26200) unless named:
- **Frame Warp:** with a test game that takes 50 ms to show a turn, streamed at 2560x1440 and 60 fps, the model locked 0.6 s after the stream's opening picture, and over two runs Frame Warp took the picture's distance from where the mouse said it should be from a median of 64.9 px to 1.4 and 2.2 px (p95 184.2 to 10.6 and 13.4 px), 96% of it removed. Its GPU passes take 0.14 to 0.16 ms a frame (median) from 1080p to 4K, and the time from present to send did not change. In Assassin's Creed Black Flag Resynced, streamed to a Retroid Pocket 6 and a Radeon 780M laptop, it learned the camera while turning and kept the HUD in place.
- **Sending:** at 4K60 HEVC, slice delivery got the picture to the client 1.2 to 1.3 ms sooner over loopback and 0.5 to 1.0 ms sooner paced as for a Wi-Fi client. At 4K60 with large frames, with early error-correction blocks a frame started leaving about 0.55 ms sooner on HEVC and AV1, and HEVC's end-to-end time fell 0.5 ms; PyroWave frames started leaving 0.13 ms sooner at 4K. At 2560x1440 and 120 fps with 16 recovery keyframes per run, an H.264 keyframe started leaving 0.04 ms sooner at the median and 0.06 ms sooner at p95 (0.45 to 0.41 ms, 0.51 to 0.45 ms).
- **PyroWave:** a 4K60 stream at 600 Mbps used 3.4 % of a core less than 3.9.0, and packetizing took 0.09 ms instead of 0.32 ms. At 3840x2160, 120 fps and 1.4 Gbps to a Radeon 780M laptop, two frames in flight measured no lower latency (median end to end 15.4 ms either way, host processing 2.5 ms).
- **Error correction:** GFNI made packetizing large frames 2 to 4 % faster on a Ryzen 7 8845HS, with the same bytes; finishing each packet 16 and 8 bytes at a time is 10-16 % faster at Moonlight's default packet size on the Ryzen 7 5800X3D.
- **Pacing:** on a Retroid Pocket 6 (59.8 Hz) streaming a 120 fps window at 60 fps, frames from the PC to the screen took 31.0 to 31.9 ms (median) and 36.2 to 36.7 ms (95th percentile) against 31.6 to 32.2 and 38.7 to 38.8 ms before the phase lock fix. Fixed-rate streams at 1080p60 with a 120 Hz game had a lower end to end p95 in every pairing: 10.66 to 10.02 ms beside a window animating at 30 fps, 12.31 to 11.44 ms on a quiet desktop, and 10.30 to 10.08 ms at 1080p120. Grid pacing at HEVC 1440p120: end-to-end p99 16.47 to 15.71 ms, picture age p99 21.0 to 19.5 ms.
- **Radeon bitrate ceiling:** AV1 at 1920x1080 and 60 fps with 1.5 Gbps asked for: the encoder ran at 1000 Mbps, the driver's AV1 ceiling at that size and rate, warned with both rates, and delivered 683 of 683 frames at 60 fps with a 2.4 ms average encode.
- **Microphone:** through the real Steam Streaming Microphone devices, speech went from PESQ-WB 1.09 to 4.64, and tones from 440 Hz to 10 kHz kept their pitch and level (THD+N -66 to -74 dB). With the slider at 100: 43% of speech samples over full scale before, none after. With packets held back 40 to 80 ms at random, silent holes in speech went from 73 (2.1 s) to 26 (0.18 s) and median PESQ-WB from 2.60 to 2.84.
- **Automatic error correction:** on a Retroid Pocket 6 over real Wi-Fi at 150 Mbps, it lost 14 frames over six runs without the floor and none over four runs with it.
- **Browsers and guests:** the play page in Chrome and Edge (1080p60 over loopback) ran H.264 and AV1 at 60 fps with no frames dropped and 9.2 to 9.9 ms from a frame's arrival to its display. Browser streams on the PC itself connected in 535 ms in Firefox and 522 ms in Chrome at `127.0.0.1`. Guests had picture within 0.9 to 2.4 s and sound within about 0.25 s; with three guests watching, the owner's AV1 1080p60 stream kept host processing at 0.37 ms mean and 1.17 ms p95 (0.44 and 1.11 ms with none). A WebRTC frame's last packet reached the client over loopback after 185 µs instead of 402 (P frame) and 1,047 µs instead of 2,825 (key frame), with 8-12% less host CPU on the PC and 4.6% less on the 780M laptop.
- **Controllers and touch:** at 500 rumble changes a second, all 3000 got through a ViGEmBus pad in a median 0.065 ms, where about a quarter had got through at 6.6 ms. A two-finger pinch took 28 to 34 µs per pass instead of 49 to 145 µs. `gamepad_feedback_fast_poll` took a VHF pad's rumble median from about 4.6 to 1.6 ms.
- **Background streams:** Assassin's Creed Black Flag Resynced through Nucleus Co-op 2.4.2 started in 4 of 6 starts before and 15 of 15 after, each launching once with the hooks loaded.
- **Displays and moves:** at the end of an exclusive stream, a TV that was off stayed off in every run, and a new stream right behind that end showed video 2.8 to 3.5 s after the quit, against 4.4 s. Continue here moved a stream from a mini PC to a Retroid Pocket 6 in about 1.9 s with the app still running.
- **Library:** listing the running programs took 1.7 ms instead of 5.6 ms with 370 programs running.
- The NVIDIA paths are covered by unit tests; no NVIDIA GPU was available to stream from.

Each release's own measurements are in its notes below and in [PERFORMANCE.md](PERFORMANCE.md).

## New in 3.11.10

- **Controllers work again where Vibepollo was uninstalled.** Uninstalling Vibepollo removes the virtual gamepad device but leaves its driver in Windows, and setup then kept that driver without setting the device up again: every stream with a controller failed with "Virtual gamepad driver unavailable". Setup and updates now set the device up again when it is missing, so installing this version fixes it.

**Tested.** On a laptop with the gamepad device removed and its driver left in Windows, the driver's own script skipped it as in the report; the device tool setup now runs set the device up again, and the controller interface answered.

## New in 3.11.9

- **Leftover setting removed.** The permanent virtual display count (`dd_virtual_display_permanent_count`, earlier `dd_vdd_static_monitor_count`), which nothing has read since permanent virtual displays were removed, is dropped from saved settings and can no longer be set per app or device.
- **Keyframes leave sooner.** H.264 keyframes no longer have the whole picture scanned one byte at a time to adjust their header: on the RX 7900 XT at 2560x1440 and 120 fps with 16 recovery keyframes per run, a keyframe's first packet left 0.04 ms sooner at the median and 0.06 ms sooner at p95 (0.45 to 0.41 ms, 0.51 to 0.45 ms).
- HEVC pictures whose header Rubylight rewrites, and H.264 keyframes, now reuse the host's pooled frame buffers instead of a fresh one each, and the video sender reuses its packet buffers block by block instead of freeing hundreds of them when a large frame follows a small one. HEVC at 3840x2160 and 60 fps, 500 Mbps: the first packet left 0.01-0.02 ms sooner, the rest unchanged.
- Error correction finishes the last bytes of each packet 16 and 8 bytes at a time instead of one by one: 10-16 % faster at Moonlight's default packet size on the Ryzen 7 5800X3D.
- On NVIDIA, a recovery frame after packet loss no longer waits in 1 ms steps for the frames already in the encoder: it waits on the encoder's own completion signal.

## New in 3.11.8

- **Native YUV 4:4:4 on NVIDIA GPUs.** A device with its YUV 4:4:4 option on (Moonlight's, or Nightjar's **Sharper colored text (YUV 4:4:4)**) now gets full color detail in H.264 (8-bit) and HEVC (8-bit and 10-bit, SDR and HDR) from an NVIDIA GPU whose video encoder reports 4:4:4 support. Colored text and thin lines keep their color at full resolution. The stream uses the bitrate the device asks for.
- At startup the host encodes a test picture in each 4:4:4 mode and offers only the ones that work. The console's host readiness lists them, for example "YUV 4:4:4 in H.264, HEVC, and HEVC 10-bit."
- AV1 4:4:4 is not available on NVIDIA: the NVIDIA encoder interface Rubylight uses encodes AV1 in 4:2:0 only. Choose HEVC for 4:4:4.
- A device that asks for a 4:4:4 mode the host did not offer gets an error naming the reason, instead of a 4:2:0 stream it did not ask for. Dolby Vision streams in 4:4:4 carry HDR10, since Dolby Vision profile 8 is 4:2:0 only.
- AMD GPUs are unchanged: their encoders make 4:2:0 only, so 4:4:4 from an AMD host still comes through PyroWave or Full chroma.
- **No false "game not detected" warning after a Playnite game.** With per-device settings files, every stream of a game started through Playnite ended with the warning that no process of the game was seen, although the files were swapped and saved correctly. Rubylight now finds the game's programs in its folder.
- **Wait after a game closes** is a new setting under Games & automation → Playnite: how long a Playnite game must stay closed before its stream ends. It stays at 15 seconds unless you change it, because a game that starts through a launcher or restarts itself is briefly reported closed. Until now the wait was fixed, and the exit timeout, which is how long a game gets to close when Rubylight ends it, did not shorten it; its description now says so.
- **Choose the time of day Rubylight looks for updates.** Set **Update check time** under All settings → General → Updates, for example 03:00. With **Install updates automatically** on, a new release installs after that check once nothing is streaming. A time that passes while the PC is off, asleep or streaming is caught up at the next opportunity; leave the field empty to keep the interval.
- **USB audio and haptics installs when Windows' PATH variable has a malformed entry.** Setup stopped at that driver with "Illegal characters in path" when one PATH entry held a stray quotation mark or another character no folder name has. The driver was then never installed, and every launch with the controller type "DualSense with waveform haptics (USB/IP)" failed. A launch whose controllers did not appear now names the missing driver instead of suggesting a reconnect.
- Above about 840 Mbps of pacing, the host's video socket buffer grows with the pace to hold 10 ms of traffic, up to 8 MB, instead of a fixed 1 MB. Lower bitrates keep the 1 MB buffer.
- New setting **Settings → Video → Codecs → Overlap PyroWave frames** prepares the next PyroWave frame while the GPU still encodes the current one, with two frames on the GPU at once. It is off by default: at 4K and 120 fps it measured no lower latency.
- PyroWave pictures go from the encoder's output straight into network packets, one full-frame copy fewer per frame.
- **Listing the running programs takes a third of the time.** The host reads the process list in one call and opens a program only once: 1.7 ms instead of 5.6 ms per list on our RX 7900 XT PC with 370 programs running. The host lists them while it tracks a game.
- The 30-second library check looks up where Steam is installed once instead of twice.
- Full chroma's description no longer says "on AMD": the setting was never limited to one GPU vendor.
- With no stream, launch or game running, the host's housekeeping wakes when there is something to do instead of checking constantly, and streams, control messages, UPnP port forwarding, the library sync and the PC's name lookups each do less repeated work.

**Tested.** The host, core and Windows unit suites passed on Windows 11, with formatting checked and Clippy run with warnings denied, and the web console's checks and tests passed. The NVIDIA 4:4:4 conversion, capability check and refusals are covered by unit tests; no NVIDIA GPU was available to stream from. PyroWave at 3840x2160, 120 fps and 1.4 Gbps from an RX 7900 XT to a Radeon 780M laptop: two frames in flight measured no lower latency (median end to end 15.4 ms either way, host processing 2.5 ms), so that setting starts off; neither this host nor 3.11.7 lost a frame on that link.

## New in 3.11.7

- A Moonlight stream that joins another device's game from the Resume or Running Game tile no longer ends that game.
- A device that may only view streams can no longer start a Remote Monitor or Remote Input session.
- A resolution switch the encoder refuses puts the display, the co-op window and the bitrate back to the mode the stream keeps.
- Ending a game's leftover processes can no longer end an unrelated program that took over one of their process ids.
- A stream above 250 fps, a sound device with only rear height speakers, a Playnite date with unusual characters, a deeply nested Steam file or a hand-written Lossless Scaling start delay could stop the host. They no longer do.
- A second stream that joins a device's display leaves that display with the device it belongs to.
- The text a host text field sends to the client's keyboard keeps its last character whole.
- A virtual controller the driver refuses to unplug keeps its slot until the session ends.
- Console: a game's "Let Lossless Scaling start by itself" can follow the host setting, and an untouched game no longer turns it off.
- Console: Updates says whose stream an Install now waits for and still offers to install without waiting.
- Console: the library marks the game that runs, not every game with its name.
- Console: Neural Rendering names what failed (install, removal or the check at launch) and keeps refreshing after it is switched.
- Setup: elevating with a folder that ends in a backslash keeps the arguments after it, and removal deletes Rubylight's service when the registered path differs only in case.
- The host reads Steam's app list again only after Steam changed it, instead of every five minutes.
- A Steam game the store has no cover for is asked about less often: after ten minutes, then an hour, then once a day.
- The console keeps the covers and icons it already loaded and only asks the host whether they changed.

**Tested.** The host, core and Windows unit suites passed on Windows 11, with formatting checked and Clippy run with warnings denied. New tests cover reading Steam's app list only after it changed, the longer waits for a cover the store does not have, and the console's unchanged-cover answer.

## New in 3.11.6

- **Wake-on-LAN with Hyper-V external switches.** The host now includes physical network adapters whose IP bindings belong to a virtual switch when choosing the MAC address sent to Moonlight and Nightjar. Previously these adapters could be missing, leaving clients with a zero or unrelated wake address.

- **PyroWave on constrained links.** The host now lowers the video bitrate when frames are lost while sending, alongside losses reported by the client. It also bounds bursts on routes without client bandwidth hints and paces recovery after congestion.
- **PyroWave conversion and packet submission.** D3D11 full-chroma streams can use compute conversion for 8- and 10-bit pictures. Conversion views are reused between frames, and short UDP frame tails can share a segmented send.
- Known issue: Steam's rumble stop can still be lost on a virtual DualSense without usbip-win2. Use usbip-win2 or DualShock 4 emulation until the virtual gamepad driver is fixed.

**Tested.** The Windows host and Windows unit suites passed, as did the core Wake-on-LAN policy tests, formatting and Clippy with warnings denied. The Wake-on-LAN inventory check reads the real Windows interface table. A real sleep/wake cycle on the reporter's PC has not been verified.

## New in 3.11.5

- **DualSense vibration stops when Steam stops it.** With the DualSense controller that has waveform haptics (usbip-win2 installed), Steam Input's Identify Controller and other Steam rumble no longer keeps the device vibrating until the stream loses focus.
- Known issue: with the DualSense from the virtual gamepad driver (without usbip-win2), Steam's rumble stop can still be lost, because the driver reports it as the previous rumble. Until the driver is fixed, install usbip-win2 or choose DualShock 4 emulation.
- **Full chroma on HEVC is no longer refused at 2560x1600 and other large sizes.** Full chroma doubles the coded height, so 2560x1600 is coded as 2560x3200. From about 6.2 million coded pixels (2560x1210, 1920x1618 and up), the host sends each HEVC frame as four slices. The check the host runs before a full-chroma stream counted each slice as a frame and refused the stream with "packed encoder failed to return frame 2". The check now counts whole frames. AV1 wasn't affected. Fixed from the reporter's log and the code; we haven't streamed it on an RX 9070.
- **Steam Controller as a controller type.** **Controller type**, for the host and for each device, now offers **Steam Controller**: games and Steam see a wired 2026 Steam Controller with both trackpads, back grips, grip and stick touch, motion and a battery. A client with one touchpad has it split into the left and right pads. A client that has a real Steam Controller and announces it also gets Steam's pad haptics and settings, and its firmware details, so Steam doesn't offer a firmware update for the virtual one; any other client gets rumble made from those haptics. Nothing changes unless you pick it. Don't start a controller firmware update from Steam while streaming: it would go to the virtual controller.
- **Updated virtual gamepad driver.** Setup installs libvirtualgamepad 0.1.0-beta.10 (driver 0.1.0.92, signed as in Vibepollo 2.0.6) over 0.1.0-beta.6. For existing pads: a virtual DualSense no longer shows up twice in Steam, as DualSense, DualShock 4 and Switch Pro now carry their real hardware IDs; the Switch Pro's gyro turns at the right speed (it ran about 5.5 times too fast), on SDL's axes, with rumble and battery levels read as on the real pad; DualShock 4 and DualSense motion timing follows the driver's clock, battery levels no longer read up to 10% high, and two fingers no longer merge into one touch; and pads survive sleep and resume more reliably. Xbox pads are unchanged. Run setup with the gamepad driver selected to update it.
- AMD encoding builds against AMF 1.5.3, which corrects how the host queries the driver's bitrate and long-term reference limits.
- AC3 and E-AC3 audio passthrough uses FFmpeg 8.1.3.
- **Less work per frame.** The host keeps colour conversion views of captured pictures between frames, waits instead of spinning while the Radeon encoder's input is full, reuses buffers for HDR metadata, and only watches the presents of the processes it streams.

**Tested.** The host and client test suites passed on Windows 11. On an RX 7900 XT, the AMF encoder tests passed with AMF 1.5.3, including the full-chroma HEVC check at 2560x1600, and AC3 and E-AC3 round-tripped through the packaged FFmpeg 8.1.3.

## New in 3.11.4

- **Windows Graphics Capture streams match each frame to its present time faster.** The host now keeps the present timestamps it receives from Windows' graphics capture in order and looks a frame's time up directly in the capture window, instead of scanning the full 2-second event log three times per frame.
- **Less work per frame and per packet.** Colour conversion, D3D11 HDR statistics readback, encrypted control messages, 48 kHz audio resampling and input and control handling each do less for every frame or packet, leaving more of the CPU for the game and the encoder.

**Tested.** The host and client test suites passed on Windows 11, including new unit tests for out-of-order DXGI present timestamps, 48 kHz zero-phase audio resampling and reordered RTP video blocks.

## New in 3.11.3

- **Rubylight no longer reserves memory inside Nucleus Co-op.** To find Nucleus Co-op's Play button, Rubylight used to reserve a little memory inside Nucleus Co-op's process and read the button's name back from it. It now asks Windows' accessibility interface (UI Automation) for the name instead. Reserving memory in another program is something antivirus software looks for, so this removes one reason a scanner might flag Rubylight. Launching through Nucleus Co-op works as before.

## New in 3.11.2

Rubylight 3.11.2 runs the Radeon encoder at the driver's bitrate ceiling instead of refusing a stream that asks for more.

- **A bitrate above what the Radeon encoder takes no longer refuses the stream.** A device that asked for more than the AMD driver's encoder accepts at that size and frame rate (Nightjar can ask for up to 2 Gbps, left over from PyroWave) got "AMF error AMF_OUT_OF_RANGE" at the start, for Full chroma AV1 already when connecting. The encoder now runs at the driver's own ceiling, reads it from the driver, and the stream's warnings and the host log say which rate it runs at. A peak bitrate ratio set in the config is held to the driver's range the same way. This applies to H.264, HEVC and AV1, and to bitrate changes during a stream (nightjar#80).

**Tested.** On the RX 7900 XT PC with Windows 11 (build 26200), an isolated host streamed AV1 at 1920x1080 and 60 fps with 1.5 Gbps asked for (1199 Mbps of video after error correction) to the independent moonlight-common-c receiver for 12 seconds. 3.11.1 ended the stream at the start with AMF_OUT_OF_RANGE and no frames. 3.11.2 ran the encoder at 1000 Mbps, the driver's AV1 ceiling at that size and rate, warned with both rates, and delivered 683 of 683 frames at 60 fps with a 2.4 ms average encode. The full test suite passed.

## New in 3.11.1

Rubylight 3.11.1 gives Dual screen mode's second screen a display of its own when a game is running, lets games capped at the stream rate go out without waiting on the phase lock, and makes NVIDIA encoding steadier under light and loading games.

### Dual screen

- **The second screen shows a second display, not a copy of the first.** Nightjar's Dual screen mode with a game running put the second screen on the game's own virtual display, so both screens showed the same picture. It now gets a virtual display of its own beside the game's. On a PC that streams its own monitors instead of a virtual display, the second screen streams the PC's other monitor; with a single monitor it shows the same one, and the stream's card says so.

### Frame pacing

- **Games capped at the stream's frame rate no longer wait on the host.** A game held to the stream rate by RTSS or its own limiter gives the host no earlier picture to take, so the phase lock could only hold each frame for the client's refresh, up to a whole frame as the two clocks drifted apart. A user's Shield TV timings showed 8 to 11 ms more latency from the hold. These frames now go out as they arrive; the log says "phase lock stands aside" when this happens. **Hold capped games for the device's refresh** (Frame pacing, advanced) brings the hold back: on that Shield it halved the rare display gaps.

### NVIDIA

- Experimental: **NVIDIA encodes at full GPU clocks.** NVIDIA's power management lowers the clocks of a GPU that a capped or light game barely loads, and NVENC slows down with them. A user's RTX 5070 took about 6 ms per 4K60 frame with the game capped at 60 fps. The host now sets NVIDIA's power management for itself to "Prefer maximum performance", in a driver profile named Rubylight that covers only the host, as Sunshine-based hosts do. An idle host uses no GPU, so the clocks rise only while it streams. Turn off Full GPU clocks while streaming (`nvenc_latency_over_power`) in the NVIDIA NVENC settings to keep the driver's own power management.
- **NVIDIA: loading screens no longer restart the encoder.** NVENC gave up on a frame after 100 ms. A game holding the GPU that long while loading restarted the encoder, froze the picture for about 0.2 s and sent a keyframe. After two of these, the rest of the session converted frames on the slower graphics queue. NVENC now waits as AMF does: the stream restarts a silent encoder after 250 ms, and waits up to 2 s if it stays silent. A user's RTX 5070 log showed two restarts in 12 s during game loading. Their next sessions will show whether it holds; we have no NVIDIA hardware to test it.
- Warnings about compute copies and conversion no longer tell NVIDIA users to update the AMD driver. Slice delivery on NVENC's D3D12 path now names the D3D12 path as the reason frames leave whole, instead of asking for a driver update.

**Tested.** On the RX 7900 XT PC with Windows 11 (build 26200) and a Retroid Pocket 6 with a simulated second display: with a game running, 3.11.0 put both screens on one virtual display; 3.11.1 gave the second screen its own, an empty extended desktop beside the game. With the virtual display off and one monitor, both screens showed that monitor and the stream's card said why. On the 780M laptop, host present-to-sent stayed at 4.3 ms (median) with and without the frame pacing change. The NVIDIA changes are untested here (no NVIDIA hardware). The full test suite passed.

## New in 3.11.0

Rubylight 3.11.0 streams a second screen to one device. With Nightjar's new Dual screen mode, a phone, handheld or PC with two displays shows a second PC screen on its other display, under the pairing it already has. Thanks to Umberto Marotta for the feature.

### Dual screen

- **A device's second screen streams under one pairing (#75).** Nightjar's Dual screen mode streams a second display to the same device next to its first stream: the host keeps it as a stream of its own, so it neither replaces the first screen's stream nor shares its display, and puts the two virtual displays back where the user left them the last time they streamed together. Both screens follow the device's permissions and settings, each keeps the resolution and scale picked on it, each screen's bitrate changes, clipboard and statistics reach its own stream, and unpairing or turning the device off ends both. Moonlight and a launch without Dual screen mode behave as before.

**Tested.** On the RX 7900 XT PC with Windows 11 (build 26200), with a Retroid Pocket 6 and a simulated second display: both screens streamed side by side on virtual displays of their own, the second at 1080p and at 720p while the first kept its size; a bitrate change on the second screen reached only its stream; the clipboard reached both; leaving the second screen kept the first and the game running, leaving the first ended the second; a 5 s Wi-Fi drop reconnected both; turning the device off in the console ended both streams within a second. The full test suite passed (4991 tests). Not run on hardware: a desktop client on two monitors, and the kept arrangement of the two displays (covered by tests).

## New in 3.10.0

Rubylight 3.10.0 gets frames onto the network sooner and with less CPU, forwards controller rumble the moment a game changes it, and puts a whole pinch into one touch frame. The console now says when an encoder setting costs the stream.

### Sending and encoding

- **The first packets of a large frame leave sooner.** An idle sender now gets each error-correction block of an H.264, HEVC or AV1 frame as soon as it is built instead of waiting for the whole frame, and PyroWave frames go out that way again. At 4K60 with large frames the first packet left about 0.55 ms sooner on HEVC and AV1, and HEVC's end-to-end time fell 0.5 ms; PyroWave's first packet left 0.13 ms sooner at 4K. Frames that fit one block don't change. `video_send_early_blocks` (default on) turns it off.
- **Less CPU for PyroWave at 4K.** Large AMF, NVENC and PyroWave frames reuse their buffers instead of allocating megabytes per frame. A PyroWave 4K60 stream at 600 Mbps used 3.4 % of a core less than 3.9.0, and packetizing took 0.09 ms instead of 0.32 ms.
- **Faster error correction on newer processors.** On CPUs with GFNI (AMD Zen 4 and newer, Intel Ice Lake and newer) the host computes error correction with it, with the same bytes: packetizing large frames was 2 to 4 % faster on a Ryzen 7 8845HS. `fec_gfni` (default on).
- **Fixed-rate pacing no longer claims a frame up to 1 ms late.** With `frame_pacing = grid` the host stopped waiting in an encoder output query close to its tick. HEVC 1440p120: end-to-end p99 16.47 to 15.71 ms, picture age p99 21.0 to 19.5 ms.
- **IPv4 clients with `address_family = both`** now count IPv4 headers in every send budget and batch size; some counted IPv6 headers before.
- **AMF output options, both off.** `amd_output_prediction` skips output queries the oldest frame can't answer yet, and `amd_output_thread` collects AMF output on a thread of its own. Under GPU load prediction gave no consistent gain; the thread cut picture age p99 by 2.5 to 9 ms but made the typical frame 0.8 to 1.7 ms later at 1440p120 and used more CPU.
- Experimental: **NVIDIA streams wake as NVENC finishes a frame.** On a D3D12 NVENC session the stream waits on the encoder's output fence instead of its 100 µs poll. `nvenc_output_event` (default on) goes back to the poll.
- Experimental: **`pacing_follow_client_link`** (default off) lifts the 800 Mbps ceiling of H.264, HEVC and AV1 on a wired route when the device reports its own link: the ceiling becomes 80 % of the slower of the two links.

### Controllers and input

- **Xbox 360 (ViGEmBus) rumble arrives at once and stops when the game stops it.** Each rumble change is forwarded as soon as ViGEmBus reports it instead of at the next 8 ms poll. At 500 changes a second all 3000 got through in a median 0.065 ms; before, about a quarter got through at 6.6 ms, and the final stop could go missing and leave the motors running.
- **Rumble no longer piles up.** Each poll takes every report a virtual pad has waiting and forwards the newest, so a game that changes rumble faster than 125 times a second doesn't build a backlog. A DualSense or DualShock 4 keeps its lightbar and trigger effects when a rumble-only report follows in the same poll.
- **A pinch is one touch frame.** All moving fingers of a pass reach Windows together: a two-finger pinch took 28 to 34 µs per pass instead of 49 to 145 µs, in one injection instead of 2 to 4.
- **USB DualSense, Steam Deck and browser input threads run at input priority,** like the stream's own input thread.
- **The control thread no longer waits on display lookups.** Input is applied before other housekeeping, the device-drawn cursor's display scale is looked up on a thread of its own, and control packets no longer copy the settings each time.
- **Input timings in the host log.** The 5 s `stream timings` line adds `input_receive_to_injected_ms` (p50/p99/max from reading a pass's first packet to its input reaching Windows) and `input_passes`, and a `gamepad timings` line every 5 s while pads are plugged shows queue times, queue depth and feedback polls. Mouse input over a loopback stream read a median 0.14 to 0.15 ms.
- **`gamepad_feedback_fast_poll`** (default off) polls the pads every 2 ms for 250 ms after a change: a VHF pad's rumble median fell from about 4.6 to 1.6 ms, at about twice the gamepad thread's CPU.
- **`absolute_left_release_delay`** (milliseconds, default 10) sets how long a left release waits after absolute mouse, touch or pen input, so a long press can right-click; 0 releases at once.
- Experimental: **`gamepad_driver_host_priority`** (default off) runs the Windows process that hosts the gamepad driver at high priority while a VHF pad is plugged in, so a busy PC delays controller input less. Other drivers in the same process run raised too; the host log names them.

### Console

- **The console says when an encoder setting slows the stream.** An AMD setting changed to a value that adds delay or weakens recovery now says what it costs under its menu, and the Settings page lists every such setting you changed, with a link to each. This covers the usages that queue frames at 4K (Driver default means AMD's transcoding profile), the Quality preset (about 1 ms more per 4K frame on HEVC and 12 ms on AV1 than Speed), pre-analysis (about 20 ms per 4K frame on 8-bit SDR streams), the low-latency modes turned off, and Maximum frame size on Driver default, where a large keyframe can outgrow error correction. The numbers are our RX 7900 XT measurements. Dismiss the list once and it stays hidden until another costly value appears.

**Tested.** On the RX 7900 XT PC with Windows 11 (build 26200), against 3.9.0: 28 paired streams of HEVC, AV1 and PyroWave at 1440p120 and 4K60, idle and under GPU load, with no encoder hang and no regression; the numbers above come from those runs. GFNI error correction produced the same bytes as before and ran on the Ryzen 7 8845HS laptop. Pads (ViGEmBus, VHF, DualSense) and touch were timed from outside the host, idle and under CPU load. Keyboard and mouse were equal under load and 4 to 8 µs slower at idle, within the drift of a fixed run order. The VHF pad's rumble can stall under the heaviest CPU load on 3.9.0 too. The full test suite passed (4969 tests). Not run here: NVIDIA (no hardware), `pacing_follow_client_link`, `gamepad_driver_host_priority`, the cursor across a display change and browser input.

## New in 3.9.0

Rubylight 3.9.0 brings back a ViGEmBus controller for tools that only see USB pads, and keeps background streams off the PC: no game sound on its speakers, and Epic games start the way their launcher starts them.

### Controllers

- **Xbox 360 (ViGEmBus) controller type.** For tools such as reWASD that look for USB controllers and may not see the standard virtual pads. Choose **Xbox 360 (ViGEmBus)** as the controller type (`gamepad = vigem_x360`) and every client gets an emulated USB Xbox 360 pad with rumble, in co-op and background streams and for a Steam Deck too. It needs ViGEmBus installed separately, which is retired upstream; without it controllers stop working and the stream says to install it, with no other pad in its place. A pad that fails to plug or unplug frees its slot, so the next client still gets one.

### Background streams

- **Background streams keep the game off the PC's speakers.** A device's "play audio on the PC" no longer brings a background stream's game sound to the PC; it still applies to that device's normal streams. Without a virtual playback device the game stays audible on the PC and the stream warns.
- **Experimental: Epic games in background streams.** A game the Epic Games Launcher installed now starts with the arguments that say which game it is, without its sign-in, so it shouldn't restart through the launcher on the PC's monitor. If its instance still exits at once, the stream says that may be why. Every user on the PC can write the launcher's manifests, so only the game's name and namespace are taken from them, from a restricted set of characters; the manifest's own launch command is ignored, oversized manifests and links are refused, and the game must be an `.exe` inside its install folder. The app's arguments can still add the launcher's options.
