**Tested.** On the RX 7900 XT PC with Windows 11 (build 26200) and ViGEmBus 1.21.442: a chosen ViGEmBus pad reached XInput, its buttons and rumble went through, Windows listed it as a USB Xbox 360 controller (`USB\VID_045E&PID_028E`), and it unplugged cleanly. We have no reWASD to try it with. New tests cover the pad's wire format against ViGEmBus's headers, a missing driver, failed plugs and unplugs, and a Steam Deck client that still gets the chosen pad; a background launch request that keeps the game's sound off the PC, end to end; and Epic manifests with links, oversized files and unexpected characters. The Epic launch has not run on an Epic game here, and the sound fix has not run in a real background stream.

## New in 3.8.0

Rubylight 3.8.0 lets you try it without giving up the host you have: setup can keep Vibepollo, Apollo or Sunshine and install beside it.

- **Experimental: try Rubylight while Vibepollo, Apollo or Sunshine stays installed.** Setup now asks when it finds another host: replace it, which still imports its settings and uninstalls it and stays the default, or keep it and install alongside (`--side-by-side` for a quiet install). Alongside, setup leaves that host, its drivers and its profile alone, and installs Rubylight's virtual display driver as usual. Rubylight gets its own service (`RubylightService`), its own ports (base 48589, or the next free range) and new settings, so devices pair again; add the PC to Moonlight or Nightjar with the address setup shows, such as `192.168.1.20:48589`. Updates, repairs and uninstalling keep to that.

**Tested.** On an AMD laptop, the packaged setup ran beside a stand-in service holding `ApolloService` and the default port: 275 checks passed across a quiet install, re-runs, an update, driver repair by command and by the service, a lost service record, a refused `RubylightService` that isn't Rubylight's and uninstalling. The stand-in was never stopped and the display driver's device reported OK. It has not run beside a real Vibepollo, Apollo or Sunshine. Not tested: two hosts streaming at once, sharing a controller driver with another host, the setup window's new choice on screen, and pairing a client through the new ports.

## New in 3.7.0

Rubylight 3.7.0 makes the couch easier: a paired device approves a new one, a running game moves to another device in one press or plays on both, and Nightjar can group Home by your Steam collections and Playnite categories. Browser streams and guest links now say why they can't connect, and Firefox streams on the PC itself. A monitor no longer ends up at 60 Hz or dark after an exclusive or shared stream, and a TV no longer lights up alone when a sleeping monitor wakes as an exclusive stream ends.

### Move or share a running game

- **Continue here.** A paired device with View and Launch can take the running game's stream from another device: `POST /session/continue-here` ends the other device's stream, keeps the game running and tells that device where its stream went for two minutes. Co-op, background, Input-only and Remote Monitor sessions and WebRTC guests are left alone.
- `GET /session` names the device playing the running game for callers with List or View.
- A press that fails changes nothing for good: the game goes back to the first device, still running, and survives close on disconnect for a minute, so that device can come back.
- A device that does not own the game may join it with `/resume` beside the owner. That `/resume` answers 409 when another device holds a plain WebRTC stream, which a Moonlight stream would end, or when its `appuuid` names another game than the running one; a request without `appuuid`, and the owner's own, resolve to the running game. It never starts a game.
- **Stream here too shows the game.** The second device now shares the display the game is on, whatever resolution it asked for: the picture is scaled to the device, and the game's resolution, refresh, HDR and scale stay as they were. If that display is slower than the device asked for or has other HDR, the stream still runs; apps that read the stream's warnings say so, and the host logs a different HDR. A plain second stream and Desktop still get a display of their own for each device. A game started from the console or opened on a physical monitor is not on a display to share, so the second device sees an empty desktop there.

### Browser streams and guest links

- The console's Stream page now works in Firefox on the PC itself. With the console opened at `127.0.0.1` or `localhost`, the host offered only that address, and Firefox doesn't send anything to a loopback address, so every stream failed after 5 seconds with "The connection to the host failed."
- A browser on the PC itself keeps its monitors on: the `exclusive` display layout, and `ensure_only_display` on a physical display, run as `extended_primary` for such a stream, since the browser showing it is on one of them.
- When a browser stream or a guest link can't connect, the page now says what it saw, in plain words: the browser never tried or the PC didn't answer at this address and UDP port (within 8 seconds), or the encrypted connection failed (within 30 seconds), each with its fix. The host's log records the same.

### Your monitors stay as you set them

- **An extended stream no longer changes your monitor after an exclusive one.** The end of an exclusive stream saved a Windows layout for that device with its virtual display as main and your monitor at the refresh Windows picks (60 Hz on a 120 Hz monitor). Every later extended stream from that device was handed it: the monitor ran slower for the whole stream, a game losing focus could put windows on the virtual display, and the layout could be learned as the device's own. Now the entry keeps your displays as you have them, and a stale one is put back when it shows up, also for a second stream's display, for a Remote Monitor's and when another stream's display leaves. A device that already learned the main-display layout this way keeps it until you change its setting.
- **A stream going on keeps its layout when another display leaves.** Monitors that were off stay off, and a game the leaving display minimised comes back. A layout you changed yourself in the meantime, a window you minimised yourself and a game running in the background are left alone, and a Remote Monitor stream or a paused one never pulls the game forward.
- **Monitors no longer stay dark after a shared session.** A layout is saved only while a virtual display it was made for is still there, also when another display arrives, and never under your own monitors' entry, so Windows has nothing with your monitor off to recall once the last stream ends.
- **A sleeping monitor no longer leaves a TV on alone when an exclusive stream ends.** A monitor that went to sleep during the stream can drop off the list for 3 to 6 s as it wakes, and with the virtual display already gone Windows switched on the only display left, such as a TV that was off. On a PC with such a display the virtual display now stays in the background until the monitor has stayed for 3 s or come back (up to about 13 s), then your layout is put back. Nothing waits for that: closing a game, ending a session or shutting down answer as before, and a new stream starts right away, ending the wait early. That stream takes the wait over: the TV goes off again, and on a layout that keeps your monitor the monitor is back at its mode once it returns. If the launch fails, your layout is still put back. Saving or restoring the display baseline and resetting display memory ask you to try again in those few seconds.

### Pairing from the couch

- **Approve a new device from a paired one.** A device with the new **Approve new devices** permission sees who waits to pair and enters the PIN the new device shows, so a TV or console no longer means a walk to the PC. The new device gets Play, never more than the approver holds. Three wrong PINs end an address's requests for five minutes, a wrong PIN holds that address for 30 seconds, and every approval raises a toast, a log line and the `pairing.paired` webhook, naming both devices. The first device with a certificate paired on a host from now on has the permission unless paired as Watch only (a browser paired earlier doesn't count), later devices get it under Devices, Other, and devices already paired are unchanged by the upgrade. It allows nothing else, and an approved device doesn't inherit it. Host control approves too.
- The pairing toast now opens the Devices page with the PIN field focused, also after signing in, and the tray has a new **Pair a device** item that opens the page at the QR code.
- **Open in Nightjar.** The Devices page has an **Open in Nightjar** link next to **Copy pairing link**, so a console opened on the phone, or on a PC with Nightjar, pairs in one click. Nightjar asks before it pairs.

### Library

- **Collections without setup.** Each library entry now names its Steam collections and Playnite categories, so Nightjar can group Home by them. Steam's files are only read; missing or changed files mean no collections and a log line, never a failed library. A file that can't be read keeps the last collections for two more scans at most.
- **Steam installs show up sooner.** With auto sync on, an install or removal reaches Nightjar in about a minute instead of up to 5 minutes. Library scans now run at background priority.

**Tested.** On the RX 7900 XT PC with Windows 11 (build 26200) and a test host. Browser streams on the PC itself connected in 535 ms in Firefox and 522 ms in Chrome at `127.0.0.1`, 527 ms in Chrome at `localhost` and 784 ms in Firefox at the PC's network address; before the fix Firefox failed after 5 seconds. With Nightjar on a mini PC as the devices, on the `extended` and `exclusive` layouts: a stale Windows layout was put back at a first stream, a second stream and a Remote Monitor, leaving the monitor primary at 120 Hz; Stream here too left the display's resolution, refresh and scale as they were; an exclusive second stream kept the physical displays off to the end apart from the moments Windows recalled a layout; and a layout chosen with Windows' own display switch during a stream was still remembered for that device, while `/extend` taught it nothing. At the end of an exclusive stream, on a monitor that dropped off the list as it woke, a TV that was off stayed off in every run, where it had lit alone for about 2 s; a new stream right behind that end showed video 2.8 to 3.5 s after the quit, against 4.4 s before. The monitor stopped dropping in the last test rounds, so those runs are from the builds before the final changes, and a next stream taking over while the monitor is away ran in unit tests only. On a Retroid Pocket 6, Stream here too joined a running stream, and Continue here moved it from a mini PC in about 1.9 s with the app still running. The full test suite passed on this code.

## New in 3.6.2

Rubylight 3.6.2 makes background streams sturdier: a game now starts once through Nucleus Co-op and keeps its controller, and a game that swaps its window as the stream starts no longer ends the stream. It also steadies HDR metadata on a picture that keeps jumping.

- **A game that replaces its window as the stream starts no longer ends the stream.** When a game swapped its splash window for its main window just as the capture opened, the capture waited five seconds for a frame the old window would never send, and the stream ended with "WGC helper did not produce a first frame". It now gives up on a closed or hidden window at once and opens the new one, and a capture that did wait the whole five seconds gets another try when the game has opened a new window. A game that hangs on its own window still fails after those five seconds, with the capture's own reason. With a test game whose window is hidden at that exact moment, the stream started about 2 s after the client connected, and about 5.5 s with only the extra try.
- **Steadier HDR metadata when a game's picture keeps jumping.** HDR10+, HDR Vivid and Dolby Vision metadata snaps to a new scene at once, so a picture that jumps several times a second moved the display's tone mapping just as often. After a cut, further jumps within a second now glide over about 15 samples. Normal play hardly changes: in a Black Flag Resynced session on the RX 7900 XT, 4 of 5 jumps in about 17,000 samples still snapped. One player reports bright UI flashing in that game with several jumps a second. We could not reproduce the flashing here, in nine setups, so whether this ends it is theirs to confirm.
- **A background game starts once and keeps its controller.** Nucleus Co-op's auto-play could start a game a second time while the first start was still running, which killed the first one. That looked like the game refusing Rubylight's input hooks, so after a few launches the game ran without them and the controller only worked while its window had the focus. Rubylight now presses Play itself, once, after the profile is loaded, and forgets what it learned from those false refusals, so those games get their hooks back. A game that really refuses the hooks learns that again, with one failed launch per way of injecting. Handlers with setup steps of their own keep Nucleus Co-op's auto-play, because Nucleus Co-op shows no Play for them. Measured with Assassin's Creed Black Flag Resynced on the RX 7900 XT: 4 of 6 starts ran before, 15 of 15 now, each launching once with the hooks loaded. With another window in front, the client's D-pad moved the game's menu.
- **An NVIDIA PC no longer reads about AMD in the wrong places.** The encoder text on the console's fallback page, Help's explanation when a GPU can't encode a codec, and the notice about NVIDIA settings left by Rubylight 2.6 now fit both kinds of GPU.

**Tested.** On the RX 7900 XT PC with Windows 11 (build 26200) and a test host, a Retroid Pocket 6 played Assassin's Creed Black Flag Resynced as a background stream through Nucleus Co-op 2.4.2 in AV1 with HDR10+, for the numbers above. Nine setups, the game's own AMD FSR frame generation among them, did not show the flashing a player reports. A test game whose first window is hidden right as the capture starts streamed in 4 of 4 starts, and in 3 of 3 with only the extra try; with no new window, the stream ended after 5 s with the capture's error. The gate passed: 4604 host tests, clippy and the release build. Not run: a two-player start through Nucleus Co-op, a handler with setup steps of its own, the laptop's real-client run, and NVIDIA hardware.

## New in 3.6.1

Rubylight 3.6.1 fixes two problems reported on 3.6.0: a device whose decoder freezes on long-term references can now turn them off for itself, and the PC's mouse no longer stays stuck to a game that refused Nucleus Co-op's input hooks.

- **Long-term reference frames per device.** Devices has a new Encoder overrides section, so one device can turn long-term references off (for a decoder that freezes on them, such as the Fire TV Cube on Moonlight) while the others keep the host's setting.
- **The PC's mouse stays free once you click away from a game that refused Nucleus Co-op's input hooks.** Nucleus Co-op told such a game every second that it was the active window, so a game like Assassin's Creed IV: Black Flag Resynced kept pulling the cursor towards its window beside the desktop. It no longer gets those messages. While the game is really in front it may still hold the mouse, since it reads the stream's controller only then: one click elsewhere on the PC, or Alt+Tab, frees the mouse and stops the controller until you click the game's taskbar icon. A game that pauses when it is not in front now stops its picture on the stream too.

**Tested.** On the RX 7900 XT PC with Windows 11 (build 26200) and a test host, a Retroid Pocket 6 streamed HEVC through AMF with the new setting saved through the console's API: Use the host setting gave 4 long-term references, Off gave none, 2 gave 2, and removing it gave 4 again. The section was checked in the console in dark and light, and on a phone width. The gate passed: 4595 host tests, 2052 client tests, 359 console tests with the build, and clippy; the warning's new wording goes through CI. Not tested on hardware yet: we have neither a Fire TV Cube nor Assassin's Creed IV: Black Flag Resynced. The Cube's reporter found that turning long-term references off cures the freeze, using the host's own setting. The mouse fix comes from the reporter's logs and is not confirmed on the game.

## New in 3.6.0

Rubylight 3.6.0 lets a TV, console or car browser play with no app: open the new play page, pair once and pick a game with the remote. A device you allow can now restart, shut down or update the PC from Nightjar, and browser streams no longer show the green rows that AV1 on AMD leaves under the picture.

### Play in a browser

- **Play from a TV, console or car browser, with no app.** With the WebRTC server on, open `https://<PC address>:47991/play` on the local network or Tailscale, pair once with the PIN the page shows (enter it on **Devices**), then pick a game with the remote, the arrow keys or a controller and play full screen. Hold Back or Select for a second for Disconnect and Quit game. The browser warns about the PC's certificate once. The page answers no address outside the local network and Tailscale, refuses connections from the PC's router, and pairs only with the PIN you type in the console, which shows the address each request came from. Measured in Chrome and Edge on the RX 7900 XT PC (1080p60 over loopback): H.264 and AV1 at 60 fps with no frames dropped, and 9.2 to 9.9 ms from a frame's arrival to its display, as on the console's Stream page.
- **No green rows under AV1 in the browser.** On AMD the encoder pads 1080p AV1 to 1082 rows and browsers show them; the Play, Stream and guest pages now leave them out, so a 1080p picture fills a 1080p screen pixel for pixel, and the pointer maps to the picture without them.
- **Holding Back or Esc opens the play menu on a real keyboard or remote.** The key's repeats closed the menu the moment it opened. In a PC browser's full screen, Esc may only leave full screen on the first hold; hold it again for the menu.

### Restart, shut down and update from a client

- **Restart and shut down the PC from Nightjar.** A new per-device permission, "Restart and shut down PC", off by default and separate from Sleep PC, lets a device restart or shut down the PC while nothing runs on it. The host refuses while a stream, a game, a browser guest or a controller session is active and says which (naming the device that streams); each request needs a fresh confirmation that expires after 30 seconds, and apps with unsaved work are never forced closed. When Windows refuses, in any display language, or the permission is turned off before the PC goes down, the device says why.
- **Update the host from a client.** A device with Host control can install the host's pending update; it is refused while another device streams, naming it, and never ends a stream another device starts afterwards: the install waits for it. Install now stays available while an automatic update waits for the PC to be idle.
- New webhooks: `power.restart`, `power.shutdown` and `update.started`. Native API: `GET /power`, `POST /power/restart`, `POST /power/shutdown`, `GET /update`, `POST /update/install` (see `docs/native-api.md`).
- The Devices page no longer says the first paired device gets every permission: its Everything role leaves out file transfer and Restart and shut down PC.

**Tested.** On the RX 7900 XT PC with Windows 11 (build 26200) and a test host: Firefox and Edge 156 paired on the play page with a typed PIN, started a game with the keyboard and played it full screen, and the Stream and guest pages played in Firefox. An AV1 1080p stream, 1082 rows from the encoder, filled a 1920x1080 browser window with no bar, gap or green row. At 2560x1440 and at 1080p, in a window and full screen, the pointer reached the PC within 2 pixels of where it was aimed, at the corners and the centre. A held Back or Esc opened the play menu, and it stayed open. Restart, shut down and a real host update from a client, and the play page on a real TV or console, are not tested on hardware yet.

## New in 3.5.0

Rubylight 3.5.0 finishes the background and co-op streams from [#59](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/59): a game's sound now leaves the PC's speakers once it opens it, a game that ignores Nucleus Co-op's input hooks gets its controller from the start, and the PC says when someone starts such a stream. UPnP now forwards the browser guest ports, Firefox opens invite links from another Rubylight PC, and fixes from users' logs bring back Nucleus Co-op on some PCs, the frame limiter after a stream and Xbox PC games in Add games.

### Background and co-op streams

- **A background stream's game no longer plays on the PC too ([#59](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/59)).** 3.4.0 moved the game to the virtual playback device as it started, before the game had opened its sound. Windows refuses that for every game ("The parameter is incorrect"), so the sound stayed on the PC's speakers. Rubylight now moves the game once it opens its sound, trying again after half a second, then less often. A game that plays on Windows' default device moves at once; one that opened a particular device keeps it until it next starts, and the stream says so.
- **A game that refuses Nucleus Co-op's input hooks takes the controller from the start ([#59](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/59)).** Such a game, Assassin's Creed IV: Black Flag Resynced for one, reads its controller only while its window is in front, and it waited for a click on its taskbar icon on the PC. Rubylight now brings its window to the front once as it opens, unless someone uses the PC. The person at the PC can click back to their own window.
- **The PC says when a device starts a background or co-op stream.** These streams play in a window of their own, so the person at the PC could not tell that someone started playing. A Windows notification now names the device and the game, once per stream: reconnecting does not show it again, and the main stream, which takes over the screen, never shows one.

### Browser guests

- **UPnP forwards the browser guest ports.** With UPnP and the WebRTC server on, the PC asks the router to forward the guest page (TCP, the base port + 2, 47991 by default) and one media port per guest (UDP `webrtc_media_port` + 1 to + 4, 8002-8005 by default), and removes them when the host stops. Guests from the internet no longer need ports forwarded by hand on a router that allows UPnP.
- **Firefox opens invite links and consoles from another Rubylight PC.** Every host's console certificate came from a root of the same name, so Firefox on a PC with Rubylight installed refused another host's pages with `SEC_ERROR_UNKNOWN` instead of the usual warning you can click through. Each host now gives its root a name of its own and makes its certificate anew once after the update; setup trusts the new root as before.
- The browser guest page has the Rubylight icon instead of a missing one.

### Games and apps

- **Nucleus Co-op runs from its folder again on PCs where Windows gave an app capability rights on `C:\`.** Rubylight counted that entry as someone who could move the folder, refused Nucleus Co-op and logged it every few seconds. Such an entry grants nothing its user isn't allowed already, so it no longer counts. A refusal that stays is logged once until its reason changes.
- **Game and Nucleus Co-op settings an earlier session changed always come back.** Putting them back no longer depends on Rubylight still running Nucleus Co-op from that folder: they come back from the chosen folder and from the copy an earlier Rubylight installed, as long as only administrators can change it.
- **The frame limiter no longer stays stuck after a stream.** When RTSS reported the restored cap in another form (the same rate with another denominator, or any denominator with no limit), Rubylight kept the limiter as pending and warned at every stream. The same cap now counts as restored. A setting this RTSS doesn't report is named once, and a different cap is still an error.
- **Xbox PC games in Add games.** A game that starts through `GameLaunchHelper.exe`, and a config saved as UTF-16, are found. A package with no application is skipped quietly. A launcher scan error now says why, not only which package.
- The missing Windows HDR Calibration warning (`virtual_display_hdr_calibration`) shows once per host start instead of at every stream.

**Tested.** On the RX 7900 XT PC with Windows 11 (build 26200), from the SYSTEM account the host runs as and from a signed-in user's: Windows refused to move a test process's sound before the process had opened its sound and accepted it after. Sound on Windows' default device moved to Steam Streaming Speakers and back, and sound opened on a particular device stayed where it was. On NTFS, the handle Rubylight holds kept the Nucleus Co-op folder and its parent from being renamed. Firefox 157 on this PC, which trusts its own host's root, refused a fresh test certificate on 127.0.0.1, localhost and the LAN address with `SEC_ERROR_UNKNOWN`; with the root's own name it showed the usual warning and loaded once accepted, and Chrome 157 and Edge 156 behaved the same before and after. The focus change with a real game, the PC notice, UPnP forwarding on a router and an Xbox PC game that starts through GameLaunchHelper are not tested on hardware yet.

## New in 3.4.0

Rubylight 3.4.0 lets friends watch or play along in their browser while you stream ([#67](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/67)), keeps each device's own game settings ([#72](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/72), [#69](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/69)), and with Nightjar 4.4.0 turns a phone or handheld into the PC's microphone ([#56](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/56)). Background games no longer play on the PC's speakers ([#59](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/59)), one exit timeout covers every app ([#71](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/71)), and full chroma is offered again after the host starts with Windows.

### Guests in the browser

- **Share your browser stream with friends ([#67](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/67)).** While you stream on the console's Stream page, **Guests → Invite** makes a link for up to four guests: view only, controller, or controller with keyboard and mouse, lasting 1 hour, 24 hours or a time you pick (5 minutes to 7 days). Guests open it in their browser, type a name and see and hear your stream; no app, account or pairing. The list shows who watches, with Kick, and every link, with Revoke. Restarting Rubylight revokes every link.
- **One encode, no relay.** The PC encodes once and each guest gets the same frames over its own connection, so a guest on a slow link drops frames alone instead of holding up yours. Guests connect straight to the PC: nothing to set up on the local network or over Tailscale; from the internet forward the guest port, TCP 47991, and UDP 8002-8005, never the console's 47990. The guest port serves only the guest page, and only while a link works ([Guests in the browser](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/blob/main/docs/browser-guests.md)).
- **Safe by default.** The link's token travels only after `#` and the host keeps just its hash; wrong links are rate-limited per address; a guest's page reaches nothing of the console, the host checks every guest input against the invite's permission, and guests' input waits behind yours, four pads each at most. Guests see a certificate warning, so keep keyboard-and-mouse links for your own network or VPN. No text or voice chat.

### Games and apps

- **Game settings per device.** List a game's own settings files in its app (Game settings per device; `%LOCALAPPDATA%` and other Windows variables work), and each device keeps its own copy: the TV its 4K settings, the handheld its 1080p ones. When a device starts the game, the host keeps the PC's files and puts that device's copy in place; when the game closes, the files are saved as the device's copy and the PC's own come back. A device's first launch keeps the files as they are and saves them for it at the end. The editor shows which devices have a copy. A missing or locked file stops the launch with the reason. While the game is still running after the app stopped (a launcher kept it open, the host restarted), nothing is put back until it closes, or until you press Put back now in the app's editor; files that cannot go back yet show a warning in the console and are put back as soon as they can. The last three PC files put back stay as backups in the host's config folder. A file cannot be both a managed game file and a per-device settings file: such a launch says so. The host does not find a game's settings files by itself yet ([#72](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/72), [#69](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/69)).
- **One exit timeout for every app.** Settings → Library → App behavior (Advanced) sets how long the host waits for an app to close before ending it, 10 seconds unless changed. An app's own exit timeout still wins. Games the Playnite sync added now follow it too: the sync no longer writes 10 seconds into each one ([#71](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/71)).
- The native library now tells clients when each game was last played, from Steam and Playnite, so Nightjar's List layout can show it for games on this PC. A game a store never played is marked so; manual apps and stores that keep no such time say nothing.
- A game without a cover of its own has none in the native library, so Nightjar shows its title instead of the grey placeholder.

### Microphone

- **Microphone only sessions** for Nightjar: a device can be the PC's microphone with no stream, through the normal microphone path (Steam Streaming Microphone, level clamp). It needs only Launch apps, not Input-only sessions; the session takes no input, starts no app and leaves the PC's sound on the PC. Listed as `session.mic_only` in the native API ([#56](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/56)).

### Background mode

- **Background games play only on the client.** In background mode and with game-only audio, the game's sound played on the PC's speakers as well as on the client. The game now plays on a virtual playback device nobody hears (Steam Streaming Speakers, or the virtual sink set in Audio settings) while the client still gets it. When Rubylight closes the game (the session or app ends), the game goes back to its own device first. A game left running after a disconnect keeps playing on the virtual device until it is started again, and one that quit by itself or outlived a host crash goes back as soon as it next starts. Nothing else on the PC changes device. In background mode the game moves as soon as Nucleus Co-op starts it, before its window shows. Windows doesn't move sound a game has already started, so a game that starts its sound before Rubylight moves it keeps playing on the PC until it is started again; the stream says so, as it does when there is no virtual playback device. Voice chat on a communications device of its own raises no warning.
- **Games that refuse Nucleus Co-op's input hooks start again.** Without hooks (Assassin's Creed IV Black Flag Resynced), Nucleus Co-op stayed on its setup screen because it no longer listed the controller. It lists it again, so Rubylight's profile can start the game.

### Streaming

- **Full chroma survives a start with Windows.** When the host started before anyone signed in, its packed 4:4:4 check failed and clients were not offered full chroma until the host restarted. The check now waits for the sign-in and runs then.

**Tested.** On the RX 7900 XT PC, the 3.4.0 build ran as a test host. Game settings per device: with a test game, a device's first launch kept the PC's settings file and saved the device's copy when the game closed. The next launch put that copy in place, while the Retroid Pocket 6, a second device, got the PC's file. A locked file stopped the launch with the reason. Killing the host mid-session put the file back as soon as it started again, and Put back now ended a stuck swap. A window that ignores closing was ended about 0.7 s after its 3 s exit timeout. Microphone only: the Retroid ran 7 min 20 s with its screen off (21,975 packets, 5 concealed), and desktop Nightjar ran 2 minutes minimized. Mute and Unmute stopped and restarted the PC's microphone, and a second device was refused as busy. Browser guests, in Chrome on the same PC and Chromium on a Linux PC on the network: guests had picture within 0.9 to 2.4 s and sound within about 0.25 s. With three guests watching, the owner's AV1 1080p60 stream kept host processing at 0.37 ms mean and 1.17 ms p95 (0.44 and 1.11 ms with none), and every guest decoded the same frames as the owner. A fifth guest was turned away, a view-only guest's input changed nothing, and Kick, Revoke and a 5-minute expiry worked. The guest port answered 404 whenever no link worked. In a release-build benchmark the owner's frames stayed within about 1 µs of no guests with one or four guests, H.264 and AV1. Keyboard-and-mouse guests, background mode with a real game and full chroma after a start with Windows are not tested on hardware yet.

## New in 3.3.0

Rubylight 3.3.0 tells clients why a stream failed, in the host's own words, and lists the host's warnings on the console's Home page. It keeps the streamed microphone clean ([#56](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/56)), keeps a monitor that keeps reconnecting from blanking the stream, takes the network and display checks off the frame path, and brings Nucleus Co-op and NVIDIA fixes.

### Errors and warnings

- **Clients learn why a stream failed.** A stream that ends on an encoder error now tells Moonlight it was the encoder, so stock Moonlight shows its encoder advice instead of a bare "Error code: 80004005". The host keeps its own words for the failure (for example that HDR is on but the display is not in HDR), and Nightjar shows them in its error dialog.
- **A stream larger than the encoder takes says so.** Asking for a resolution beyond what AMF or NVENC can encode (8192x8192 on the RX 7900 XT, whose AMF HEVC limit is 8192x4352) now ends with that limit and the advice to choose a lower resolution, instead of a message about signing in or updating the driver. A size the encoder cannot take is no longer retried on the graphics queue either.
- **A refused launch names its cause.** When the encoder setting doesn't fit the PC (for example NVENC on an AMD-only PC), the client now gets the host's reason and the fix ("Set the encoder to Automatic"), not "No video encoder is ready".
- **Host warnings on Home.** The console lists the host's own warnings (settings replaced after moving from Apollo or Vibepollo, the removed NVIDIA frame limiter setting, missing codecs, a Windows Firewall rule that blocks Rubylight) with a Dismiss button. A one-time notice stays until you dismiss it, also across restarts; a dismissed warning comes back only when its text changes.
- A Windows Firewall rule that blocks the host is now a warning on Home and a finding in Help & diagnostics, not only a log line.

### Streaming

- **A monitor that keeps reconnecting no longer blanks the stream over and over.** With the exclusive layout, a monitor that drops its signal in standby or scans its inputs came back every 30-60 seconds, and each time the host switched it off again, which blanked the stream. After the same monitor comes back three times within five minutes, the host now leaves it on for the rest of the stream and says so on the stream card.
- **Frame timing keeps working after a refresh change.** On WGC capture, a game or Windows changing the display's refresh during a stream left the host timing frames against the old refresh, which could turn the present-time correction off for the rest of the stream. The host now reads the refresh again and starts the correction over.
- **Fewer Windows calls in the frame path.** The route to the client (network adapter and its speed) and the display's colour and HDR state are now checked on their own threads every two seconds and every second, as before, instead of on the encode thread before a frame goes to the encoder and on the video sender between frames. The capture thread no longer waits behind a display check either. The 5 s `stream timings` log line shows what is left (`stage_checks_ms`) and the slowest route lookup (`link_lookup_max_ms`).
- The route lookup skips the adapter's traffic counters, which it never used.
- `video_send_mmcss_only` (off by default) runs the video packetizer and sender with only the multimedia scheduler's boost, as the input thread does, for measurements under CPU load.

### Microphone

- **The streamed microphone no longer clips into noise.** Windows' recording slider for Microphone (Steam Streaming Microphone) runs up to +30 dB, with 0 dB at 54: at 100, or wherever a voice app's automatic gain pushed it, the client's voice reached apps 30 dB too loud and turned into distorted noise. While a client's microphone plays, Rubylight now keeps that slider at 0 dB or lower, checking again every second on a thread of its own, apart from playback, so the level set in Nightjar is the level apps hear. A lower level stays as you set it. Measured through Steam Streaming Microphone on the RX 7900 XT PC with the slider at 100: 43% of speech samples over full scale before, none after, tones back at 0.0 dB from +30 dB.
- **A late microphone packet no longer breaks the voice off.** When packets arrive in bursts, as over a busy Wi-Fi link, the playout used to run dry and cut the voice into silence for 10 to 80 ms. It now fills the gap with the Opus decoder's concealment, fades it out if the gap lasts past 40 ms, and crossfades back into the voice when its packets arrive. The concealment never plays ahead of what silence would have, so the delay stays the same, and playback never waits for the network thread's decoder. Measured through Steam Streaming Microphone on the RX 7900 XT PC with packets held back 40 to 80 ms at random, nine runs each: silent holes in speech went from 73 (2.1 s) to 26 (0.18 s), median PESQ-WB from 2.60 to 2.84, and the time trimmed to keep latency down from 3.3 s to 3.1 s. The "microphone stopped" log line now also reports how much was concealed and any concealment that failed.
- The host log now names Steam Streaming Microphone's playback and recording formats each time a client's microphone starts, and Rubylight reads the playback format back after setting it, so a format Windows doesn't keep shows up as a warning instead of distorted sound.

### Nucleus Co-op

- **Background streams survive a game's start (Experimental).** A game that swaps its splash screen for its main window, or resizes its window, while the stream opens no longer ends the first stream: the capture tries again on the game's current window for a few seconds. When the capture helper ends during start, the stream now says why instead of "Access is denied" or "The handle is invalid".
- **Unreal games fill the phone's screen (Experimental).** A game Rubylight adds to Nucleus Co-op itself starts in a window at the stream's resolution (`-ResX`/`-ResY` with `-ForceRes`, such as 2400x1080 on a 20:9 phone) instead of at its saved 16:9 resolution, even when that is larger than the PC's monitor, unless its arguments choose one.
- **No title bar in the stream (Experimental).** A game window with a title bar or sizing border (Split Fiction) loses it, so the stream shows the game alone at the stream's size.
- **The PC's keyboard and mouse stay out of DirectInput games (Experimental).** For a game whose .exe imports DirectInput, Rubylight's Nucleus Co-op handler now also keeps the PC's raw input from the game, so NieR:Automata no longer reacts to keys pressed on the PC. Other games keep their raw input, so a pad they read through it still works.

### NVIDIA

- **NVIDIA: bitrate changes no longer stall or force a keyframe.** A higher bitrate from adaptive bitrate or error correction used to wait for the frames in flight and send a keyframe; on drivers that change the bitrate in place it now applies at once, as on AMD.
- **NVIDIA: a clear message when the encoder sessions are taken.** When another app such as OBS, ShadowPlay or Discord holds the GPU's encoder sessions, the stream now says so instead of suggesting a driver update or sign-in, and the startup check no longer opens a second session beside the first.
- NVIDIA: AV1 now starts on drivers from 522.25, and native 4:4:4, which the colour conversion cannot feed, is refused with a clear message.

**Tested.** On the RX 7900 XT PC with an isolated host, HEVC 2560x1440 at 120 fps and 3840x2160 at 60 fps streamed at 119.8 to 120.0 and 60.0 to 60.1 fps with no warnings. Moving the network and display checks off the frame path measured within run-to-run spread against 3.2.2 over eight alternating 90 s legs at 1440p120 (host time p99 1.19 against 1.24 ms, encode p99 3.98 against 4.04 ms; the base legs alone varied by 0.16 ms), so we claim no speed-up, and `video_send_mmcss_only` was not better under full CPU load, so it stays off. Two HDR toggles mid-stream were picked up on both builds. Requesting 8192x8192 ended with the encoder's limit in the host log and in Nightjar's dialog, `encoder = nvenc` on this AMD PC with the NVENC reason at launch, and a Windows Firewall block rule showed on Home. The release build ran 4248 Rust tests and 316 console tests without a failure. NVENC changes are not tested on an NVIDIA card.

## New in 3.2.2

Rubylight 3.2.2 answers a Microsoft Defender warning on 3.2.1: setup no longer unpacks programs into ProgramData, the service keeps the same file across updates, Rubylight starts no hidden PowerShell for its own work and no longer downloads Nucleus Co-op. It also brings NVIDIA GPUs back with NVENC (Experimental), sound after a late audio ping, and a controller type per device.

### Antivirus warning

- **Setup no longer unpacks programs into ProgramData.** Windows Defender's cloud protection quarantined the Rubylight service while setup had it unpacked in `C:\ProgramData\Butterpollo\setup`. Setup and updates now unpack, and in-app updates download the installer, into a `.staging` folder inside the Rubylight folder, which only administrators can change, and remove it when they finish; a failed update keeps its backup there for the rollback. Setup deletes the old `ProgramData\Butterpollo\setup` folder, and Rubylight the installers earlier versions downloaded to its `updates` folder.
- **The service file stays the same while the service version is unchanged.** `butterpollo-service.exe` now has its own version and changes only with it, so after an update antivirus programs see a file they already know instead of a new one. The service starts and watches the host, watches the display driver and rolls back interrupted updates as before. It asks the host about the driver only when the device appears, before a repair, or again later when a check failed, and the Nucleus Co-op restore runs as the host starts and as you sign in. The checks it starts end with the service, so none of them holds Rubylight's files during an update.
- Every Rubylight executable now names its company, copyright and original file name in its file properties.
- **Fewer hidden PowerShell runs.** The Wake-on-LAN check and fix, the setup's network adapter list, its removal of the old libvirtualdisplay driver and the restore of the Remote Desktop firewall rules after child sessions now ask Windows directly (WMI, SetupAPI, pnputil and the registry) instead of starting PowerShell in the background, which antivirus heuristics treat as suspicious. The Wake-on-LAN check also works where PowerShell is blocked by policy.
- **Rubylight no longer downloads Nucleus Co-op.** The game page's Play together panel and Help link to the Nucleus Co-op 2.4.2 release instead: unpack it into a folder under C:\Program Files and choose that folder there. Rubylight checks the folder before it keeps it and says why when it refuses one: no NucleusCoop.exe, another release, a folder that anyone but administrators may change, or files Windows still marks as downloaded (unblock the zip before unpacking it). A Nucleus Co-op an earlier version downloaded keeps working. A folder chosen earlier that holds another release falls back to that copy, and the panels say so.

### NVIDIA

- **NVIDIA GPUs stream again (Experimental).** Rubylight encodes with NVENC on NVIDIA graphics cards: H.264, HEVC, and AV1 where the card's encoder offers it, with HDR, HDR10+, HDR Vivid, Dolby Vision and 4:4:4. Setup and the host accept NVIDIA. With the encoder set to automatic, the host picks NVENC or AMF from the graphics card that captures. Intel graphics stay on Rubylight 2.6.x.
- NVENC keeps frames within the same frame cap as AMF. An oversize scene cut is encoded again as a keyframe, so it keeps its error correction. With slice delivery on, a large picture's slices leave as they finish.
- NVENC encodes from the compute-queue colour conversion on a D3D12 session (NVIDIA driver 522.25 or newer) and takes encoder focus from it. The startup focus check needs driver 531.61 or newer.
- On NVIDIA the host runs at realtime GPU priority, so encoding keeps up with a game that fills the GPU. This applies with hardware-accelerated GPU scheduling on, unless you turn off Realtime GPU priority with hardware scheduling (`nvenc_realtime_hags`) in the NVIDIA NVENC settings. Without administrator rights the host runs at high priority and the log says so.
- If Rubylight 3.0 to 3.2.1 changed your encoder setting from NVENC to AMD AMF, set Encoder back to Automatic to stream from an NVIDIA card. Those releases also removed the NVENC settings, so set them again.

### Streaming

- **Sound comes back after a late audio ping.** A stream whose client's audio ping missed the first 10 seconds stayed silent until you reconnected. The host now keeps waiting, shows a warning while the ping is missing, and sends sound as soon as it arrives.

### Devices

- **A controller type per device.** Devices → device → Controller sets the virtual controller for that device alone, in place of the Controller type in Settings. Moonlight on a Nintendo Switch reports no controller type, so Automatic can't pick Switch Pro for it; now it can be set there without changing every other device's controller.

Tested. On the RX 7900 XT PC, `rubylight-setup-3.2.2.exe --quiet` installed over 3.2.1 with exit code 0: the host reported 3.2.2, the service 1.0.0 ran, the display self-test as SYSTEM passed, and `C:\ProgramData\Butterpollo\setup` and the `.staging` folder were gone afterwards. Microsoft Defender with security intelligence 1.463.76.0 found no threat in the setup, the zip or the service. Three service builds from different host sources, the last with NVENC, gave the same file (SHA-256 `ed539d9b…e975`). The native Wake-on-LAN reading matched Windows' own network adapter cmdlets on this PC (same adapters, 247 driver settings). The release build ran 4323 Rust tests and 314 console tests without a failure. NVENC is not yet tested on an NVIDIA card.

## New in 3.2.1

Rubylight 3.2.1 lets Stream in the browser pick AV1, HEVC or H.264 and stream in Firefox ([#66](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/66), [#67](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/67)), keeps fixed-rate streams steady when a game runs faster than the stream, lets pause and resume commands run side by side, and fixes the Wake-on-LAN check in the console.

### Stream in the browser

- **Stream in the browser picks the codec, resolution and frame rate.** The Stream page now offers AV1, HEVC and H.264 at every resolution and frame rate the host's encoder takes, limited to what the browser can receive. It starts with a codec the browser decodes in hardware at 1080p60 and remembers your choice ([#67](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/67)).
- **Firefox streams in the browser.** Firefox refused the host's offer with "SIPCC Failed to parse SDP: Min is not smaller than max". The page now leaves out the Samsung TV line that caused it ([#66](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/66)).

### Streaming

- **Steadier fixed-rate streams beside other windows.** When a game runs at two to four times the stream rate (120 Hz at 60 fps), the host now follows the game's own frame timing, so another window's updates no longer take the place of the game's frames or hold back the frames after them. Measured on the RX 7900 XT at 1080p60 with a 120 Hz game: beside a window animating at 30 fps, end to end p95 was 0.6 ms lower and picture age p95 1.0 ms lower; on a quiet desktop, 0.9 and 1.9 ms lower. Frames ready for the encoder waited under 0.5 ms at p95 in most runs, against 1.4 to 2.3 ms before.

### Apps

- **Pause and resume commands no longer block each other.** Rubylight waited for each pause or resume command to finish, so a script that keeps running while the game is paused (Apollo's auto-pause script with `pause.ahk` and `resume.ahk`) held back the resume and was killed after two minutes. These commands now start without waiting, as on Apollo.

### Console

- **Wake-on-LAN check reads the adapter again.** Settings → This PC showed "Allow this device to wake the computer" and "Only allow a magic packet" as Unreadable on every PC, so no adapter ever showed as ready and the console never offered to switch them on. It now reads both, and offers the fix when they're off.

**Tested.** On the RX 7900 XT at 1080p60 with a 120 Hz game, alternating old and new builds for 30 s each, fixed-rate streams had a lower end to end p95 in every pairing: 10.66 to 10.02 ms beside a window animating at 30 fps, 12.31 to 11.44 ms on a quiet desktop, and 10.30 to 10.08 ms at 1080p120, where the host claimed about 0.2 % fewer frames (119.8 to 119.9 fps against 120.1). Firefox 159 rejected the host's real offer exactly as reported in #66 and accepted it with the page's edit, answering H.264 and AV1; AV1 and H.264 at 1080p60 streamed through the Stream page from an isolated host. The full host suite, clippy and the release build pass.

## New in 3.2.0

Rubylight 3.2.0 fixes background streams through Nucleus Co-op from the reports in [#56](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/56) and [#59](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/59): quitting ends the game, your controller is the one the game reads, and the game stays off your monitors. It also makes the client microphone sound right on PCs with Steam Streaming Microphone, gets 4K streams to the client sooner, and keeps a minimum of recovery data in Nightjar's Automatic error correction.

### Background and co-op streams

- Quitting a background stream's game on the device now ends it, also after the stream itself already ended: Moonlight's Quit and Nightjar's Quit (Close game) stop Nucleus Co-op and the game instead of leaving them running for two minutes. ([#56](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/56))
- **Your controller reaches the background game again.** A background stream started soon after another one now unplugs the earlier session's virtual controllers first, so the game reads your controller instead of a leftover one. When one of the PC's own controllers sits ahead of yours, the stream says so.
- A new Nucleus Co-op session, co-op or background, first unplugs the controllers that ended streams still keep plugged in, so the game no longer reads one of those as player one. Controllers stay plugged in when the launch is refused, when the device goes back to its own session, or when Nightjar is still preparing them for a launch. ([#56](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/56))
- Nucleus Co-op's profile is now clicked even when another window covers Nucleus Co-op on the PC: the host raises it for the click without taking the focus and puts it back. The first click also waits until Nucleus Co-op 2.4.2 can load the profile, which avoids its untitled "The profile can't be loaded" box. ([#56](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/56))
- A background stream whose game window doesn't come back when you return (the game minimised or lost it) now ends with the reason after 90 seconds, so the next background launch is no longer refused while the game runs on unseen. ([#56](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/56))
- **The PC's keyboard and mouse stay out of a background game.** Its window no longer gets keys, clicks, the wheel or cursor positions from the PC, even when the game comes to the front. A game that reads raw input can still see the PC's mouse while it is in front.
- **Background games stay off your monitors.** Unreal Engine games start in a window, so they stay where Rubylight parks them instead of going fullscreen on the PC's monitor. A game that keeps coming back to a monitor, or minimises itself when it loses the focus, is put back for the whole stream, not just the first few seconds.
- A Nucleus Co-op stream of a game that waits on a "Mandatory ASLR" box of its own no longer stays on a black screen. Assassin's Creed IV Black Flag Resynced shows only "Disable Mandatory ASLR". The host now spots that box and ends the stream with the reason. When Windows forces "Force randomization for images" on the game, the message says how to turn it off for the game's .exe in Windows Security's Exploit protection. The host never changes that setting itself, and it doesn't drop the game's input hooks over it. When Windows doesn't force it, the game rejected the way Rubylight hooked it: its next start tries the next way, and as a last resort starts it without hooks. ([#59](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/59))

### Microphone

- **The client microphone sounds like itself again.** Steam Streaming Microphone passes what the host plays straight to its recording side without converting, and with its default formats (stereo 48 kHz playing, mono 44.1 kHz recording) a voice reached games and chat apps pitched down to less than half and chopped up. The host now gives the playing side the recording side's format before it plays. Measured on the RX 7900 XT through the real devices: speech PESQ-WB 1.09 → 4.64, tones from 440 Hz to 10 kHz arrive at their own pitch and level (THD+N -66 to -74 dB). If the format can't be set, the session shows a warning instead of sounding wrong quietly.

### Streaming

- **4K streams arrive sooner.** H.264 and HEVC streams with 4K-size pictures (3840x2160, 4096x2160, 5120x1440) now send each frame slice by slice while the encoder finishes the rest, with no client change; Moonlight and Nightjar reassemble the frames as before. Smaller pictures still go out whole, because there it measured no gain. `video_slice_delivery = off` turns it off, and `2` or `4` turn it on for every size. Measured on the RX 7900 XT at 4K60 HEVC: the picture arrived 1.2 to 1.3 ms sooner on loopback and 0.5 to 1.0 ms sooner paced as for a Wi-Fi client (October: 1.4 ms over Wi-Fi 6E to Nightjar on a laptop). On these streams Moonlight's overlay shows about 2 ms more host processing time, although the picture arrives sooner.
- Nightjar's Automatic error correction now keeps at least 10 % recovery data. Before, it dropped to none after one clean second, and on real Wi-Fi (Retroid Pocket 6, 150 Mbps) any lost packet then cost a frame and a keyframe: 14 frames over six runs, against none with the PC's own setting. With the floor, four runs on the same link lost no frames under either choice. The PC's setting stays the default, because under steady loss Automatic still lost more frames.

**Tested.** On the RX 7900 XT through the real Steam Streaming Microphone devices, speech went from PESQ-WB 1.09 to 4.64, and tones from 440 Hz to 10 kHz kept their pitch and level (THD+N -66 to -74 dB). At 4K60 HEVC, slice delivery got the picture to the client 1.2 to 1.3 ms sooner over loopback and 0.5 to 1.0 ms sooner paced as for a Wi-Fi client. On a Retroid Pocket 6 over real Wi-Fi at 150 Mbps, Automatic error correction lost 14 frames over six runs without the floor and none over four runs with it. The Nucleus Co-op fixes have regression tests for each failure in the #56 and #59 logs; they haven't run on a live stream of the reporters' games yet. The full host suite, clippy and the release build pass.

## New in 3.1.1

Rubylight 3.1.1 keeps streams timed to your screen's refresh after the PC was busy, starts background streams of games Nucleus Co-op was never set up for ([#56](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/56)), gets each frame of a WebRTC stream onto the network more than twice as fast, and does less work per frame across the host.

### Streaming

- **Streams no longer stay a few milliseconds behind after the PC was busy.** With Nightjar, the host times frames to arrive just before the client's screen refreshes (phase lock). When the PC was busy for a while (a build, a game loading), the phase lock could lose that refresh and never find it again: until the stream restarted, frames arrived anywhere in the refresh, about 7 ms later on average and with some judder, and the PC sent slightly more frames than the screen shows. Rubylight 3.1.0 already had this. The host now notices when it has lost the refresh and finds it again within a few seconds, also when the PC's clock and the client's run well apart.
- **Games running faster than the stream reach the screen in time.** A game at 120 fps streamed at 60 fps gives the host a new picture only every 8.3 ms, so the phase lock could only move frames a whole picture later, never earlier, and could end up with most frames just missing the refresh and waiting a whole extra one (about 14 ms). The host now moves those frames by whole pictures, a picture later when they would miss the refresh, so they reach the screen within a picture before it.

### Co-op and background streams

- **Background streams no longer stall on "Press a button on Player1's gamepad".** Nucleus Co-op decides per game whether pads are assigned by slot or by a button press, and saves the first game it ever loads with button press. Such a game never started in a background stream, since nobody can press a button before the stream is up. Rubylight now gives the game pads by slot before each background or co-op stream.
- When Rubylight can't clear out an old copy of a user's Nucleus Co-op files, the log now names Windows' actual reason instead of "The directory name is invalid".

### Performance

- **WebRTC streams (browsers and Samsung TVs) hand each frame to the network 2.2-2.7x faster, with 5-12% less host CPU.** Rubylight now writes each run of equal-sized packets to Windows in one call instead of one call per packet.
- Under the hood, the host's largest source files are split into focused modules, and the video packet path, encrypted control messages, browser streaming and Nucleus Co-op folder copies do less work per frame or file. Writing a user's own copy of Nucleus Co-op (a 127 MB folder in our test) takes a median 0.6 s instead of 1.1 s.

**Tested.** On a Retroid Pocket 6 (59.8 Hz) with Nightjar 4.1.1, streaming a 120 fps window at 60 fps with Lowest latency, the 3.1.0 host sent 60.4 to 61.1 fps and 3.1.1 sent 59.7 to 60.0 fps, locked to the screen; frames from the PC to the screen took 31.0 to 31.9 ms (median) and 36.2 to 36.7 ms (95th percentile) against 31.6 to 32.2 and 38.7 to 38.8 ms, and 0.15 to 0.96% of frames were more than half a refresh off against 1.1 to 1.6% (two 35 s runs each). On the RX 7900 XT with a game running, a WebRTC frame's last packet reached the client over loopback after 185 µs instead of 402 (P frame) and 1,047 µs instead of 2,825 (key frame); alternating old and new builds, 4K60 HEVC and AV1 WebRTC streams used 8-12% less host CPU on the PC and 4.6% less on the 780M laptop. Against 3.1.0, 1440p120 HEVC streams over Desktop Duplication and WGC and 4K60 HEVC and AV1 WebRTC streams kept the same timings, and after the refactor all eight alternating 1440p120 HEVC runs held 120 fps with the same host times. The full host suite, clippy and the release build pass.

## New in 3.1.0

Rubylight 3.1.0 polishes the console from sign-in to the last settings page, lets you remove several games at once and bring them back ([#65](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/65)), and fixes background streams through Nucleus Co-op, bursty microphones and setup with a leftover settings folder ([#56](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/56), [#59](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/59), [#61](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/61)).

### Console

- **A calmer, more consistent console.** The sidebar fits any window: its links scroll while the logo, your PC and Sign out stay in view, and it carries the same ruby mark as the tray and setup. Page buttons sit on the title's line on every page, and notices, folding sections, empty pages and buttons look and behave the same everywhere, in light and dark, on a PC or a phone.
- **Home gets to the point.** One View checks that scrolls to the checks, its own icon for your devices, and no empty gap on phones. Signing in and first-run setup are one welcoming card.
- **Games look like a library.** A game without cover art gets a poster of its own, tinted by its name and showing its initials ("Hades II" reads H2); Desktop and Steam Big Picture get a picture. Each cover has one round Launch button that shows on hover or keyboard focus, and always on touch screens. The toolbar is tidier, and Playnite shows once in the launcher list, with its options and plugin version. A game's page and Add games read more calmly and fit a phone.
- **Allow pairing from the Devices page.** When pairing is off, one button turns it on right there. Each device shows one status (Connected, Offline or Disabled), with Settings and a More menu beside it.
- **Wake-on-LAN puts the fix first.** One status for the panel, then the problem and Apply changes, with the adapter details as quiet text.
- Help reads as one page with even button groups and offers Return to console only when it can work. API tokens and Stream in the browser say what to do next when there is nothing to show yet.
- The Games page no longer breaks when a launcher scan answers without a game list.

### Games library

- **Select games in the library and remove them together.** Select on the Games page puts a checkbox on every cover; pick games one by one, all at once, or every game of one launcher, then Remove. Removed games stay out of your library: no sync adds them back.
- **Changed your mind? Restore removed games.** Under Where your games come from, Restore removed games lists the games you removed. Pick some or all, and the ones still installed are back in your library.

### Co-op and background streams

- **One stuck file no longer blocks every background stream.** After a background stream, a file in your own copy of Nucleus Co-op (a game's profile or Rubylight's handler) could end up one Windows no longer let your account change, and from then on every background stream of any game failed with "Access is denied". Rubylight now checks those files before each start, and when one is stuck it sets that copy aside and makes a fresh one. What it can't remove of the old copy is tried again at the next start.
- **Games that refuse Nucleus Co-op's input hooks get another way in.** Rubylight's own one-player handler has Nucleus Co-op inject ProtoInput into the running game, which keeps it from the PC's keyboard and mouse. Some games refuse that (Assassin's Creed IV Black Flag Resynced does), and Nucleus Co-op then shows "ProtoInput failed to runtime inject" and ends the game. The stream now ends saying so, and the next start has Nucleus Co-op inject the hooks as it starts the game, before any of its code runs. A game that refuses that too gets them from one of its own threads, and only a game that refuses every way starts without them: Nucleus Co-op still tells it it has focus, but a keyboard, mouse or pad on the PC can reach it too, and the stream says so. Rubylight remembers each game's way.
- **Reinstalling leaves your own copy of Nucleus Co-op alone.** A reinstall reset the permissions of everything in `C:\ProgramData\Butterpollo`, also each account's copy of Nucleus Co-op, and took away your account's right to change its copy: the next background stream had to copy Nucleus Co-op again. A link you made in your copy stopped every reinstall and update. Setup now leaves those copies as Rubylight made them, and Rubylight keeps checking them itself before each start.

### Microphone

- **Microphone audio from devices that send it in bursts no longer drops in chunks.** Some Android TVs deliver several voice packets at once; the host cut the queue back to 20 ms each time and lost up to 5% of the audio. The host now learns how far apart packets keep arriving and holds up to twice that (at most 150 ms). A steady device keeps the delay it had, and a device clock that runs fast loses small crossfaded slices instead of whole chunks.
- `microphone stopped` in the log now counts underruns, where the microphone ran dry while audio kept coming, and their length next to `trimmed_ms`.

### Setup

- **A permission repair that stopped halfway is finished by the next setup or update.** Settings folders from early versions belong to the account that installed Rubylight, and setup repairs them. One interrupted after the folder itself was fixed left the rest as it was, and later runs didn't notice. Setup and updates now check everything in the folder, except the Nucleus Co-op copies, and repair until all of it is Rubylight's. A settings folder that disappears while setup checks it now stops setup instead of being made again with the permissions every account has in ProgramData.
- **Setup no longer stops on a leftover `C:\ProgramData\Butterpollo` that your own account owns.** With no Rubylight installed, setup can't tell who made that folder, so it doesn't use the settings or pairings in it. It moves the folder to `Butterpollo.untrusted-<time>` unchanged, starts with new settings and says where the old folder went.
- A stream to a virtual display no longer logs a frame limiter warning when the limiter is off on purpose; the warning stays for a cap that was asked for and failed.

**Tested.** The console was checked page by page in light and dark, at 1440x900 and on a 390 px phone: against fixture data (52 screenshots), and against a running 3.1.0 host on the RX 7900 XT with its own profile, where first-run setup, sign-in and all eleven pages loaded with that PC's library of 27 games and no script errors. The console's 299 unit tests and its five browser suites pass. The full host suite (4,108 tests), clippy and the release build pass.

## New in 3.0.1

Rubylight 3.0.1 fixes co-op and background streams through Nucleus Co-op, which in 3.0.0 hung at the start and then failed with "the game did not start" ([#59](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/59)).

- **Background streams through Nucleus Co-op start the game.** Rubylight's profile left each player's processor setting empty, which Nucleus Co-op 2.4.2 can't read, so a start failed just after the game's process came up. The profile now says "any processor", as Nucleus Co-op itself writes it.
- **A click on the profile that comes too early no longer stalls the start.** Nucleus Co-op answers a click made while it still builds its setup screen with "The profile can't be loaded" and then ignores every later click. Rubylight now closes that box, logging what it said, and clicks the profile again until the game starts; once Nucleus Co-op has hidden its game list to play, it never clicks again.
- A start that times out now says where Nucleus Co-op stopped (its setup screen still waiting for a player's controller, or the box it showed after the last click) instead of asking you to load the profile by hand.
- The console says where WebRTC pairing requests appear: on the Devices page.

**Tested.** On the RX 7900 XT with Nucleus Co-op 2.4.2 running as the signed-in standard user, as in #59: Rubylight closed the early box, clicked again, and the game's process and window came up about 11 s after launch; its window was captured at 1280x720 and pad input reached it, with the session still running after a 30 s hold. The full test suite, clippy and the release build pass, with new regression tests for the profile, the re-click and the boxes it closes.

## New in 3.0.0

Rubylight 3.0.0 is the stable release of the 3.0 line. It covers everything since 2.6.0, including the nine previews from 3.0.0-alpha.1 to rc.5. It brings split-screen co-op and background streams through Nucleus Co-op, a picture that holds through scene cuts and lost frames, a bitrate that follows the network, Dolby Vision, HDR10+ and HLG, sound for receivers and soundbars, Neural Rendering, and a simpler console. Stock Moonlight clients keep working and get the streaming fixes too. Formats and features that need the client's support go only to a client that asks for them, and the protocol version is unchanged. Nightjar 4.0 is the matching client.

### Highlights

- **The picture holds through loss.** Scene cuts no longer freeze the stream (15 freezes to 0 in our test), and H.264 and HEVC repair a lost frame with a small frame instead of a keyframe.
- **The bitrate follows the network.** When the link can't carry the stream, the host lowers the bitrate and comes back by itself, PyroWave included.
- **Play together.** Split-screen co-op gives each player their own copy of the game, and background streams play away from your monitors, both through Nucleus Co-op.
- **Neural Rendering.** A switch on a game's page installs a neural upscaling mod into games with DLSS, FSR or XeSS, and takes it out again.
- **Dolby Vision, HDR10+ and HLG.** HDR streams carry per-scene brightness data, Dolby Vision works on HEVC and AV1, and screens that show only HLG get HLG.
- **Sound for your receiver.** PCM up to 7.1.4, Dolby Digital and Dolby Digital Plus, plus 7.1.4 surround, night mode, headphone surround and haptics from game audio.
- **A simpler console.** Home walks you through pairing and playing, Games lists every launcher, Settings shows the ten settings most people change, and pairing takes a QR code.
- **Clipboard, files and USB.** Copied text and images go both ways, shared folders and file transfer reach the PC's files, and USB devices can be forwarded to the PC.

### Upgrading from 2.6.x

Rubylight 3.0 needs an AMD Radeon GPU with a working AMF encoder, on Windows 10 or 11; NVIDIA and Intel graphics stay on [Rubylight 2.6.0](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/releases/tag/v2.6.0). Setup upgrades 2.6.x, Vibepollo and Butterpollo in place and keeps your settings, paired devices, games and covers.

### Picture and encoding

- **Scene cuts no longer freeze the picture.** When a scene cut comes out of the AMD encoder too large to keep its error correction, the host encodes it again as a keyframe that fits. In our 250 Mbps HEVC 1440p60 test at 0.3 % random loss, lost scene cuts went from 15–16 to 0, with no change in encode time. [Numbers](PERFORMANCE.md#october-28-lost-scene-cuts-and-recovery-keyframes-past-four-fec-blocks).
- **Recovery keyframes keep their error correction.** The keyframe that repairs a lost frame is kept small enough for its full error correction, so random loss no longer takes it too. In the same test, freezes over 100 ms went from 4 to 0 and picture age dropped by 3 ms. When keyframes still come out more than a tenth over the automatic frame cap, the host lowers that cap until they fit.
- **Faster repair after a lost frame on H.264 and HEVC**, for every client. Instead of a full keyframe, the host sends a frame predicted from an older picture the device still has. At 1440p120 that frame was 3 % to 46 % smaller than the keyframe when 1 or 2 frames were lost; for longer losses the host still sends the keyframe. `amd_ltr_frames = 0` turns it off.
- **AV1 repairs without intra refresh.** With intra refresh, the AMD driver turned off the references a repair needs. Now a loss costs a 48 KB repair frame instead of a 101 KB keyframe, and ordinary frames are about a third smaller (33.5 KB instead of 51.2 KB at 1440p120 and 50 Mbps). [Numbers](PERFORMANCE.md#october-27-av1-intra-refresh-and-recovery-after-a-lost-frame).
- **Fresher frames with VRR.** The host now times the game's own frames instead of each capture, and waits for the game's next frame when it is close. A 120 Hz game in a 60 fps VRR stream reaches the encoder 0.27 ms old instead of 4.7 ms (median) and leaves the host 3.5 ms sooner. [Numbers](PERFORMANCE.md#october-26-vrr-claims-follow-the-games-pictures).
- **A focused VRR game keeps its own pace beside another window.** On Windows 11 24H2 and newer, another window's animation or a pointer move no longer makes a focused game repeat and skip frames. With a 60 Hz game at 60 fps on a 240 Hz VRR desktop and another window animating beside it, repeated game frames fell from 159–294 to 0–20 per minute, and capture age p99 from 6.2–16.5 ms to 0.4–6.1 ms. A game alone on the display is paced as before. [Numbers](PERFORMANCE.md#october-30-a-focused-games-pictures-beside-another-window).
- **Three stream presets.** **Stream preset** under Streaming picks Lowest latency, Balanced (the default, which changes nothing) or Reference quality, for the host, a device or an app. A value you set yourself always wins. Lowest latency made the first keyframe 29 % smaller on HEVC and 38–40 % smaller on H.264, with latency within 0.15 ms of the others. [Stream presets](../docs/configuration.md#stream-presets).
- **Full chroma takes effect when you save it**, with no host restart. Turning it off, or turning off HEVC or AV1, withdraws it at once. `/serverinfo` lists the packed 4:4:4 modes the host offers (`Packed444Modes`), and a slow first frame no longer gets packed 4:4:4 refused.
- **10-bit SDR on request.** A client that asks for a 10-bit format with `hdrMode=0` gets a 10-bit SDR stream, packed 4:4:4 included.
- **PyroWave is there when you need it.** It recovers when the GPU device it converts on is lost, the host checks again a minute later when its first check timed out, and a host started without administrator rights offers it again (its check took 24 s there instead of 1.4 s).
- **Slice delivery** (`video_slice_delivery = 4`, off by default). The host starts sending an H.264 or HEVC frame while the rest of it is still encoding. At 4K60 HEVC and 80 Mbps the picture arrived 1.3 to 1.5 ms sooner on loopback and 1.2 to 1.4 ms sooner over Wi-Fi 6E. At 5 % loss, 8 frames could not be rebuilt with it on and none with it off, so it stays off by default. [Numbers](PERFORMANCE.md#october-23-slice-delivery-b2-step-2f).
- **Encoder focus** (`encoder_focus`, off by default). `static` gives still parts of the picture, such as a HUD or text, more of the bitrate; `centre` also favours the middle of the screen. A codec that fails the startup check streams as before.
- **A resized game window streams at its new size**, also when the game stopped drawing after the resize.
- **Where frames go missing.** The console's stream details, the 5 s `stream timings` in the host log and Nightjar's overlay show how many frames per second the display produced and the host captured, encoded, sent and repeated.
- **Missing codecs are explained.** When a codec is missing, the host log, the console and a refused launch say why: a setting, the driver's error by name, or a video engine without that encoder. Nothing is switched off by GPU name.

### Network and bitrate

- **The bitrate steps down when the link can't carry it** (`link_rate_adaptation`, on by default). When the device loses frames that error correction can't repair, the host cuts the bitrate to what the link delivered, probes back up in small steps and returns to the requested rate after ten clean seconds. On a 50 Mbit/s link asked for 75 Mbps it cut within 1.6 s and lost 1.1 to 1.2 % of frames after the start, instead of 6.7 %. A link that carries the stream sees no change, and the stream card says "Bitrate limited by the network". [Details](../docs/configuration.md#lower-bitrate-when-the-link-cannot-carry-it).
- **PyroWave's bitrate follows the link too.** Through a link limited to 250 Mbit/s, a 400 Mbps PyroWave stream used to lose 32 % of its packets with no picture arriving whole; now it settles at 250 to 290 Mbps, loses 2.7 % and gets 81 % of pictures through whole. It stops at PyroWave's lowest workable bitrate (187 Mbps at 1080p60), and below that the warning says to use HEVC or AV1.
- **Error correction the device chooses.** A device can pick its own share of recovery packets, or have the host follow its once-a-second loss reports. On a shaped link modelling 60 Mbit/s Wi-Fi, following the device cut median loss from 2.37 to 0.26 % and freezes from 21 to 1. The PC's own setting stays the default, and `fec_max_percentage` (50 % by default) caps both. [Numbers](PERFORMANCE.md#october-27-client-error-correction-and-a-link-test-before-the-first-bitrate-nuc).
- **No slow pace by mistake.** A clean stream no longer falls to the slow pace for the session, or for the next day, because a decoder asked twice for a keyframe ([#59](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/59)), the PC's own network adapter refused packets as a game started, Alt+Tab or a bitrate change sent a keyframe ([#48](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/48)), or the first keyframe was lost. A device asking again for the same repair no longer lowers the bitrate either. Pacing history saved by older versions is dropped once.
- **Wi-Fi pacing from the first frame.** A device that reports a Wi-Fi or mobile route (Nightjar does) gets adaptive packet pacing from the start. On a 2.4 GHz laptop link it cut unrecoverable frames from 61 to 15.7 per 90 s run, with no change on clean 5 GHz. A device that reports its wired link speed is paced for the slower port. [Numbers](PERFORMANCE.md#october-25-wi-fi-pacing-hint-on-a-real-laptop).
- **Bitrate and frame rate change mid-stream**, without reconnecting and without a keyframe, for a device that asks at launch.
- **Keep streaming when the network changes.** A phone moving from Wi-Fi to mobile data keeps its stream after proving its new address with the stream key (`client_roaming`, on by default).
- **Control messages arrive right after an outage.** What the host sent during a short outage now reaches the device 85 ms after it is heard from again, instead of 1.0 to 2.9 s later. Every Moonlight client benefits.
- **Replies come from the address the device reached** ([#54](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/54)), which fixes reconnects failing with error -1 when Tailscale runs beside the LAN.
- **Video packets are the size the device asks for** ([#52](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/52)). A Moonlight asking for small packets on a remote route got a green, broken picture; the `packetsize` setting is gone.
- **Bandwidth test.** A paired device can time a short download before it picks a starting bitrate (`bandwidth_probe`, on by default; never while a stream runs).
