On the Statistics overlay, a screen that stays at another refresh rate than the frame rate vote now shows the likely cause (no such mode, battery saver, heat, the Android refresh rate setting, or a switch that would blank the screen), and HDR asked for but not delivered shows its reason (the screen or its current mode has no HDR10, the PC reports SDR, no mastering data, the decoder's output is not HDR). HDR is requested only when the screen reports HDR10 in its current mode; HLG alone no longer counts. [Details](https://github.com/RamazanKara/nightjar/blob/main/docs/expected-hdr10.md).
- **The host lowers the video bitrate when the network cannot carry it** (`link_rate_adaptation`, on by default). When a device loses frames that error correction cannot repair, for example 150 Mbps asked of a 100 Mbps link, the host cuts the bitrate to what the link delivered, says so in the log and on the stream card ("Bitrate limited by the network: 9 of 15 Mbps"), probes back up in small steps and returns to the requested rate after ten clean seconds. A link that carries the stream sees no change. Off for PyroWave, with Host adaptive bitrate, and once a client sets the bitrate itself. Changing the bitrate never forces a keyframe on H.264, HEVC or AV1; H.264 cannot encode below about 5 Mbps at 1080p60 whatever the target (its quantiser limit), so a cut there stops at that. [Details](../docs/configuration.md#lower-bitrate-when-the-link-cannot-carry-it).
- **A client can be told when the host lowers the bitrate for the network** (new control message `0x5538`, [documented](../docs/control-messages.md#0x5538-link-notice)). A client that asks gets a short notice on each change, at most one a second and never on the video path: the requested and the current bitrate, and whether the host is probing back up or at its lowest. Nightjar says so in the stream overlay ("Host lowered the bitrate to X Mbps: network"). A bitrate that only drifts inside one state is sent at most every 5 seconds, so the line does not flicker. Clients that do not ask hear nothing, and the protocol version is unchanged.
- **A Radeon profile for each game.** In the app editor, **Radeon** picks Competitive (Anti-Lag on), Smoothness (the game's frame rate capped at the stream's), Quality (anisotropic filtering and the driver's anti-aliasing enhancement) or Custom (up to 24 settings you list). The host writes them when the game starts, through the same journaled path as the Radeon controls of the Session API, and puts the original values back when the session ends, also after a crash. A setting the driver does not offer is shown in the session and in the host log; the game still starts.
- **The session says when a launcher is in the way.** If the game does not appear and a Steam, Epic Games, EA app or Ubisoft Connect window wants an update or a sign in, the owner's session carries a `launcher` note (`session.launcher`) that a client can show. The host only reads window titles every 2 s; it never clicks or types into a launcher.
- **Microsoft Store and Xbox PC games in Add games.** The host lists the games of the registered packages that ship a `MicrosoftGame.config`, plus games in `XboxGames` folders, and starts them the way the Start menu does. Games without a package for this Windows user are shown as unavailable with the reason. Console-only titles are left out.
- **A Stream preset control in the device editor** sets the preset for one device.
- **Uninstalling no longer leaves the Intel libvpl license folder of 2.6.x behind** in `C:\Program Files\Rubylight`. Only folders Rubylight itself filled are removed; empty folders of your own, and everything when the install folder is a drive root, stay.
- **The display driver is repaired when it goes missing while the service runs**, not only at service start. The service checks every minute and repairs after the driver has been gone for a minute, never during a stream (a host that cannot say whether it streams counts as busy), never for a device you switched off in Device Manager, and at most once a day per version. A host bound to a LAN `bind_address` cannot say whether it streams, so it is not repaired by itself. To repair it at once run `butterpollo-setup.exe --repair-drivers --now` from the Rubylight folder in an administrator shell: it shows progress and the result and is not held back by the daily limit or `--no-display-driver`.
- **The startup encoder probe no longer raises an "AMF returned no output" warning** when the driver is slow on its first frame; it is logged at info.
- **Lower latency with VRR on a game slower than the display.** With VRR the host used to claim each captured picture on arrival, so a 120 Hz game in a 60 fps stream reached the encoder 4.7 ms old (median). The host now waits for the game's next frame when that frame is at least 2 ms away, as it already did without VRR: measured on an RX 7900 XT, the picture is 0.27 ms old at the encoder and leaves the host 3.5 ms sooner (median), with no change in shown frame rate. A source at the virtual display's 1000 Hz is claimed on arrival as before. [Numbers](PERFORMANCE.md).
- **A lost first keyframe no longer slows the next stream, and a client on a slower port is paced for it from the first packet.** Only loss that lasted is remembered now (the stream's first keyframe lost with nothing else wrong is held for 10 clean seconds, then forgotten), the normal rate is tried every second stream instead of every fifth, and history is kept per host network interface and client link, so another network adapter starts fresh. A client that reports its wired link speed (Nightjar) caps conventional-codec pacing at 4/5 of the slower port, but never below 1.1 times the encoder bitrate. Records saved by earlier releases are ignored. See [Packet pacing](../docs/configuration.md).
- **Missing codecs and older Windows builds are explained, not silent.** Experimental on hardware we have not run (RX 5000, 6000 and 9000, Polaris and Vega cards, Windows 10, Windows 11 before 24H2). When the AMD encoder check finds a codec missing, the host log, a console warning and a refused launch now say why: a setting, the driver's error by name (`AMF_NOT_SUPPORTED (10)` instead of `10`), or that the GPU's video engine has no such encoder (no AV1 before RDNA 3; no encoder on RX 6300 to 6500). Nothing is switched off by GPU name; the check still decides. An AMF driver older than the host's AMF version is told apart and retried with its own version. Windows APIs newer than Windows 10 are gated by their build with a logged reason: Windows Graphics Capture's cursor, border and update-interval switches no longer fail a stream on a build without them, Desktop Duplication goes to the legacy call below 1709, and the virtual display driver, which needs IddCx 1.10 (Windows 11 23H2 or later), is off by default on Windows 11 21H2 and 22H2 as on Windows 10, with a console warning where a launch asks for it. That changes one default: on Windows 11 21H2 and 22H2 the virtual display was on by default but could not load the driver.

**Tested.** The workspace tests (core 856, host 487, Windows 471 plus 20 fixture tests, setup 94, plus the smaller crates), clippy, the web console checks and the release build pass on this build. On an RX 7900 XT (Windows 26200):
- **Loopback streams of 30 s:** H.264, HEVC and AV1 at 2560x720 60 fps and PyroWave at 1080p60, every frame decoded at 60 fps. Median end-to-end time 4.6 ms (H.264), 7.0 ms (HEVC), 4.1 ms (AV1) and 11.5 ms (PyroWave).
- **Short soak:** all 67 cases that run on a release build passed (codec switches, GPU load, reconnects, the WGC helper fault), with no growth in memory, handles or threads. The three fault-injection cases need a debug host and were not run.
- **Link rate:** on a 50 Mbit/s link asked for 75 Mbps, 1.17 and 1.08 % of frames lost; behind a 400 ms queue that never drops, p95 283 and 336 ms instead of 389 and 400 ms; a clean Wi-Fi stream made no change. The runs are in [PERFORMANCE.md](PERFORMANCE.md#october-24-a-lossless-standing-queue-link-fix-delay-signal).

On a Ryzen 7 8845HS laptop (Radeon 780M):
- **Update over 3.0.0-beta.1:** the service stays automatic and running, and the host state is unchanged.
- **Virtual display:** a 60 s stream on its own virtual display, 917 frames and none dropped, captured from the driver's frame push, with the laptop panel at its 2880x1800 120 Hz mode before, during and after. Switched off by Windows mid-stream, the display came back and the capture reopened within 0.2 s.
- **Driver repair:** with the service running, a removed display driver was set up again about a minute later; a second removal the same day was left for the manual `--repair-drivers --now`, which repaired it; a disabled driver was left alone.
- **Not tested yet:** phones on this build, and Windows giving the virtual display a new identity during a stream (covered by tests only). The display self-test's "beside duplicated TVs" check still fails on the laptop, where Windows moves the panel when the stand-in TVs arrive; it passes on the RX 7900 XT.

## New in 3.0.0-beta.1

- **Three stream presets: Lowest latency, Balanced and Reference quality.** One setting, **Stream preset** under Streaming, fills in the AMD encoder settings that nobody set. A value you set yourself in the settings, for a device or for an app always wins over a preset, and a device or an app can name its own preset; the host refuses to save a name it does not know. Balanced is the defaults, so nothing changes unless you pick another. Lowest latency limits every frame, recovery keyframes included, to one frame's share of the bitrate (a one-frame cap cut the largest 4K40 HEVC recovery frame by 66%). Reference quality removes the size limit on recovery keyframes, which makes them larger on the wire, for a fast, steady link; we measured no picture quality gain from it, and H.264 with a one-frame cap was not measured separately. No preset changes the rate control: constant bitrate scored slightly higher on one clip only because it spends more of the budget, so it is not a quality setting. The console says which settings have a value of their own, and shows a saved preset name the host cannot read as an error instead of as Balanced. See [Stream presets](../docs/configuration.md#stream-presets) for the values and the measurements behind them.
- **Experimental: send H.264 and HEVC frames slice by slice** (`video_slice_delivery = 4`, off by default). The encoder hands each frame over in four slices, and the host starts sending the first one while the rest is still being encoded. It needs no client change and works with stock Moonlight. At 4K60 HEVC and 80 Mbps it brought the picture 1.3 to 1.5 ms sooner on loopback and 1.2 to 1.4 ms sooner over real Wi-Fi 6E (a laptop running Nightjar desktop). With a 160 Mbps pacing cap emulating Wi-Fi, the gain was only 0.5 to 0.7 ms. At 1% loss frame loss was no worse, and picture quality dropped by at most 0.00024 SSIM. At 5% loss, 8 frames could not be rebuilt with slices on, against none with it off. Moonlight's statistics show about 2 ms more host latency with it on, because the host time now includes the encode it overlaps with sending, even though the picture arrives sooner. AV1, PyroWave and keyframes are always sent whole. Phones have not been measured yet. See [PERFORMANCE.md](PERFORMANCE.md#october-23-slice-delivery-b2-step-2f).
- **A device keeps the Windows display scale you pick, not only the resolution** ([#36](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/36)). With **Remember the resolution I pick** on, setting 200% (or any scale) on the stream's virtual display in Windows now comes back with the next stream, applied after the display is at the kept resolution. A **Virtual display scale** set in the host settings still wins, and nothing is kept then. A scale Windows refuses is logged as a warning and the stream goes on. The refresh rate is not kept: the stream's rate (and VRR) always decides it.
- **A display layout you set for a device is never replaced** ([#40](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/40)). At the end of a stream the host saved the layout the stream's display was left in as the device's own, and that replaced the layout you had picked under Devices; a game's mode switch or Windows moving the primary display was enough, so the next stream came back as "Extended". The device editor now marks a layout you pick, and the host never learns over a marked layout, also during a stream or across a reconnect. A device without a layout of its own still keeps the layout you leave a stream in, so a game that moves the primary display can still be learned there; a layout saved by an older version is not marked either. For such a device, pick the layout under Devices once, or set it back to the host setting to clear it.
- **PyroWave recovers when the GPU device it converts on is lost.** A lost D3D12 compute device left the frame's capture slot held, so PyroWave marked the whole GPU unusable and every later stream failed with "PyroWave is unavailable on this adapter" until Rubylight restarted. The loss is now treated as a device loss, the slot is freed and the stream rebuilds on a fresh device. Tested by removing our own D3D12 device between frames, before the conversion and during a frame.

**Tested.** The workspace tests (core 676, host 443, Windows 447, plus the smaller crates), clippy, the web console checks and the release build pass on this build. On an RX 7900 XT (Windows 26200):
- **Stream presets:** 13 loopback streams of 60 s, HEVC 4K60 at 80 Mbps and H.264 1080p60 at 20 Mbps, read back the encoder settings each preset should apply. With Lowest latency the first keyframe was 29% smaller on HEVC and 38 to 40% smaller on H.264. The median end-to-end time of the three presets was within 0.15 ms, below the run-to-run spread, so a preset changes keyframe size, not latency.
- **Slice delivery:** loopback A/B runs of at least 7,000 frames per arm, alternating off and on with the same loss seed, at 0%, 1% and 5% loss. Then 8 alternating runs (18,000 frames per arm) from a 780M laptop over Wi-Fi 6E running Nightjar desktop, joined with the host's timing record. No overflow, decoder error or encoder hang in any run. The numbers are in the entry above and in [PERFORMANCE.md](PERFORMANCE.md#october-23-slice-delivery-b2-step-2f).
- **Display scale, display layout and PyroWave device loss:** covered by host, web and GPU tests, including removing our own D3D12 device at three points of a frame.
- **Not tested yet:** phones (slice delivery and presets), recovery keyframes under load with each preset, and a real driver reset (TDR) during PyroWave, which still marks the GPU unusable until restart. This is a beta; [PERFORMANCE.md](PERFORMANCE.md#october-23-stream-presets-b3) has the preset numbers.

## New in 3.0.0-alpha.3

- **Per-frame timing record.** Set `BUTTERPOLLO_TIMING_RECORD=<folder>` on the host and each video stream writes `host-<stream>.csv` with every frame's capture, convert, encode, packetize and send times on one clock ([docs/timing-record.md](../docs/timing-record.md)). While it is unset nothing runs and nothing is written. The host timing report (control message 0x5536) now also carries capture and convert times and PyroWave's stamps, and stays within what Nightjar 0.8.0 accepts. `rust/release/timing_report.py` joins the host record with a client's frame log into a per-stage table. With the next Nightjar, the client's latency log gains the host columns and a clock offset from 0x5537.
- **Updates keep the service's startup type.** Setup used to set the Rubylight service back to Automatic on every install and update. It now keeps Manual, Disabled and delayed start as you set them, and only a new service starts automatically. A disabled service is left disabled and not started, and setup says so. When the service is stopped, **Start Rubylight.exe** now offers to start it after a Windows permission prompt instead of only showing an error; declining keeps the old message.
- **Alt+Tab and bitrate changes no longer switch a device to Wi-Fi pacing for 24 hours** ([#48](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/48)). A keyframe the host sends on its own (a bitrate change, a restarted capture or encoder) was taken for the keyframe the client's next request "asked again" for, and the client's reaction to the host's change counted as video loss. The start of a stream did the same: the client asks again about 100 ms after the first keyframe, before it has shown a picture, and that second request was read as a lost keyframe. Requests and ordinary-frame reports within a second of such a keyframe (half a second of the stream's first) no longer count, even when the client asked just before the keyframe; keyframes sent to recover from loss open no such window. A keyframe the client really could not rebuild, a request repeated after the window, and repeated unrecoverable frames still count, and so do frames the host could not send whole when the send buffer fills (error 10055 at a high pacing rate): three within two seconds, or one keyframe, as before, now named "lost on send" in the log and counted once with the client's report of the same frame. The log now names the frames behind a "pacing like a Wi-Fi route" decision and the evidence it set aside.

**Tested.** All 1,645 workspace tests, clippy and the release build pass on this build. On an RX 7900 XT (Windows 26200):
- **Timing record:** 18 loopback streams of 60 s at 1080p60 across H.264, HEVC, AV1 and PyroWave. End to end, from game present to client present, the median was 7.4 ms for H.264, 8.8 ms for HEVC and AV1, and 13.3 ms for PyroWave. With the record off and on, H.264's present-to-send p99 was 11.85 and 11.78 ms, so the record costs nothing measurable. Four runs failed when Windows reset the audio device mid-stream, with the record both on and off.
- **Startup type:** an elevated test creates a real throwaway service and checks that a new one is Automatic, that Manual and Disabled survive setup's reconfigure, and that a Disabled one is not started.
- **Wi-Fi pacing:** the tests replay the stream start and the host-keyframe sequences from a real host log, and check that neither switches pacing or writes the 24-hour memory. A lost keyframe, a repeated request after the window, frames lost to a full send buffer and steady loss with reference-frame invalidation all still switch it.
- **Not tested yet:** the Alt+Tab case with a real phone, the timing record over the LAN and on phones, and the launcher's permission prompt. This is an alpha; [PERFORMANCE.md](PERFORMANCE.md#october-23-per-frame-timing-record-baselines) has the timing numbers.

## New in 3.0.0-alpha.2

- **Radeon settings from the couch.** `GET /api/native/v1/radeon` lists the Radeon settings of the GPU the host encodes on, each in one of five states: configurable, read only, needs setup in AMD Software, not supported, or temporarily unavailable. A device with host control that owns the running session can change them with `POST /radeon/settings`. Every change is written to a journal on disk before it is applied and is put back when the session ends, the game is closed, the grace period runs out, the device is unpaired or disabled, the host shuts down, or at the next start after a crash. It is not put back at Disconnect. A setting you changed on the PC in the meantime is left alone, and a restore that fails is logged and retried.
- **Native API v1 reference and compatibility guarantee.** [docs/api.md](../docs/api.md#native-api-v1-paired-clients) now documents every `/api/native/v1` endpoint: authentication with the paired certificate and the Host control flag, the error codes, versioning and the Radeon restore rules. Stock Moonlight and Nightjar 0.7.x are unaffected by clients that read the native API or have an action refused. A successful End session or Close game changes `/serverinfo` (`currentgame`, `state`) as a stock quit would. The host tests pin their `/serverinfo` (apart from `NativeApiVersion`, 0 over plain HTTP or unpaired and 1 for a paired HTTPS device), `/applist` and `/cancel` answers byte for byte, and check that a stock `/cancel` still needs only Launch permission.
- **Use it from Nightjar 0.8.0.** [Nightjar 0.8.0](https://github.com/RamazanKara/nightjar/releases/tag/v0.8.0) shows these settings in its Session and graphics page while you stream, with the reason when one can't be changed and a note on when it goes back. A Radeon setting now reports `writable_now` only when the device may also write it, so a device without View permission no longer sees a switch that the host then refuses.

**Tested.** The workspace tests, clippy and the release build pass on this build. On an RX 7900 XT (Windows 26200):
- **Couch loop with Nightjar 0.8.0** on a Retroid Pocket 6 over Wi-Fi and on the Android emulator: pair, Host control, Home, Play, Anti-Lag on from the Radeon panel, End session. Anti-Lag was back to Off within 2 s.
- **Crash test:** with the host killed mid-session right after a Radeon write, the next start restored the setting within 1 s and cleared its journal. On a virtual display, the virtual monitor left 0.2 s after the kill and the physical display's mode and position did not change.
- **Old and new clients:** Nightjar 0.7.18 against this host and Nightjar 0.8.0 against Rubylight 2.6.0 both paired, streamed and quit through the classic grid.
- **30-minute HEVC 1080p60 soak:** all 108,360 frames decoded at 60.2 fps, with one long arrival gap (max 32.4 ms), no encoder hang and no growth in host or encode time.
- **Not tested yet:** stock Moonlight on a phone. This is an alpha; [PERFORMANCE.md](PERFORMANCE.md#october-22-30-alpha2-gate) has the full numbers.

## New in 3.0.0-alpha.1

- **Rubylight 3.0 needs an AMD Radeon GPU.** Setup (interactive, quiet and update) and the host stop on a PC with no AMD GPU or no working AMF video encoder, and say why in plain words. NVIDIA and Intel graphics stay on [Rubylight 2.6.x](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/releases/tag/v2.6.0). An AMD GPU whose driver has no AMF encoder is told to update the Radeon driver. There is no GPU-generation floor and no Windows-build floor: every AMD GPU with a working AMF encoder is accepted, on Windows 10 and 11. On a PC with an AMD iGPU beside an NVIDIA dGPU, the host encodes on the AMD adapter, or names it when the monitor is on the other card. A Moonlight client that cannot launch because no encoder is ready now sees the same advice.
- **The host is AMD-only from 3.0.** NVENC, Quick Sync, and the FFmpeg software encoders (x264, x265, SVT-AV1) are removed, along with their settings, console controls and libraries. AMF encodes H.264, HEVC and AV1, and PyroWave keeps its own encoder. Settings that named a removed encoder migrate to `amf` or `auto` and the host reports the change once. NVIDIA and Intel users stay on the 2.6.x line.
- **The GPU fence ring is now the default for virtual-display capture.** Direct driver push (`capture = idd-push`, and automatic capture of a display this host created) uses the fence ring unless you turn it off. The keyed-mutex ring stays available with `idd_push_fence_ring = false`. An old display driver, or a capture without in-place compute conversion, keeps the keyed-mutex ring and the host warns in the log and the console. See "rust/PERFORMANCE.md, October 22" for the loaded comparison.
- **Nothing else is dropped.** Every AMD GPU with a working AMF encoder stays supported, older generations included, on Windows 10 and 11. Physical displays, WGC and Desktop Duplication capture keep working beside virtual displays, so you can still stream the PC's own monitor.
- **Native API v1 for Nightjar.** A paired client on the HTTPS streaming port can read the host's capabilities, the installed-games library with stable ids and covers, and the session state, and can Disconnect, End session or Close game, under `/api/native/v1`. `/serverinfo` gains `NativeApiVersion` (1 for a paired HTTPS request, 0 otherwise); nothing else in the Moonlight endpoints changes, so stock Moonlight and older Nightjar builds work as before. The contract is in [docs/native-api.md](../docs/native-api.md).
- **Host control is a per-device permission.** Devices → device → Other → "Host control" lets that device close games through the native API. It is off for every device until you turn it on in the console, and nothing on the streaming port can turn it on.
- **Sessions have explicit states.** Disconnect leaves the game running and the session suspended for the disconnect grace period, End session ends the stream but keeps the game, and Close game also quits it. Repeated or racing requests are answered, not doubled, and a host that stopped in the middle of a session reports it at the next start.

**Tested.** The workspace tests, clippy and the release build pass on this build. On an RX 7900 XT (Windows 26200):
- **Against 2.6.0, ABBA:** 56 streams covering physical-display WGC and Desktop Duplication (HEVC and AV1, 1440p120) and the virtual display with both driver rings. Every mean picture age was within 0.15 ms of 2.6.0, and there was no encoder hang.
- **30-minute HEVC 1080p60 soak:** all 107,814 frames decoded at 60.1 fps, with no encoder hang and no growth in host or encode time.
- **Upgrade:** the alpha.1 setup upgraded an installed 2.6.0 in place and kept all 12 paired devices.
- **Over wired LAN from a second PC:** a moonlight-common-c client paired and streamed HEVC 1080p60 for 60 seconds. Every frame decoded, the arrival interval p99 was 20.9 ms, and the host processing mean was 0.45 ms.
- **Not tested yet:** stock Moonlight on a phone. This is an alpha; [PERFORMANCE.md](PERFORMANCE.md#october-22-30-alpha1-hardware-gate) has the full numbers.

## New in 2.6.0

- **A simpler console for everyday play.** Home leads with setup, ready, streaming or attention status, followed by your game shelf. Home, Games & apps, Devices and Settings form the main navigation; Help & diagnostics holds logs and recovery tools. The refreshed console uses comfortable spacing, larger controls, light/dark/system appearance and a compact mobile drawer.
- **Import installed games from six launchers.** Games & apps → Add games scans Steam, Epic Games, Battle.net, EA app, Ubisoft Connect and GOG. Search, filter, select and rescan installed games, with Already added states and per-launcher scan results. Imports preserve user edits and distinguish games from different stores. Steam and Playnite synchronization and manual editing remain available.
- **Clearer pairing and device access.** Enter the streaming client's PIN in one field, then wait for the actual paired-device confirmation. Each device shows its connection state and access level, with an explicit Change access action. Other pairing methods and custom permissions are expandable.
- **Everyday settings first.** Six settings groups show common controls and their dependencies, with specialist fields under Advanced. Search finds configuration keys and advanced controls; changed-setting review, reset, unsaved-change protection and restart confirmation remain available. Game editors put the name, program or Desktop selection and cover first.

**Verification and limits:** Frontend checks and production build, 47 frontend unit tests, 61 affected browser tests, 549 core tests and 44 targeted Windows/host tests passed before release preparation. Desktop, tablet, mobile, both themes, keyboard navigation, 200% reflow and loading/error/empty states were checked. Launcher discovery uses fixtures; real-device pairing, live streaming and real store-launcher handoff still need hardware verification.

## New in 2.5.13

- **Bound the wait for a predicted capture.** When the next predicted capture does not arrive, its detection allowance now stays inside the existing prediction budget. In the deterministic 90 fps reproduction, the fallback claim moves from 13.89 ms to 11.61 ms after the previous claim, reducing the extra wait by 2.28 ms. Fresh captures can still replace the pending frame.
- **Move large frames to the existing sender before pacing their packets.** The handoff now accounts for the actual encoded frame size, packet headers, encryption and the frame's FEC, rather than relying only on the average bitrate. A large keyframe can therefore avoid holding up capture and encoding while its packets are paced. Wire-rate limits and queue bounds are unchanged.
- **Include the latest background-stream and process-audio fixes from main.** Background isolation stays fixed across reconnects, waits for its own display instead of falling back to the PC's monitor, and keeps window and audio tracking tied to the launched game. Process audio activation now preserves parameter ownership and retrieves the audio interface on its audio thread.

**Verification and limits:** Regression tests cover missed predictions at 59.94, 90, 120, 144 and 240 fps, large recovery frames on a gigabit link, and encrypted and unencrypted sender handoffs with packet numbering preserved. The 2.28 ms result is a timing-policy reproduction, not a measured end-to-end improvement. The reported RX 9070 to RTX 2050 setup and its camera-measured latency spread have not been retested.

## New in 2.5.12

- **Experimental full-chroma HEVC and AV1 for AMD.** A reversible packed 4:2:0 layout preserves every quantized luma and chroma sample before hardware compression, with 8-bit SDR and 10-bit HDR modes. Enable the experimental packed 4:4:4 option on both Rubylight and Nightjar 0.7.18. It is off by default; stock clients retain their existing modes. Compression remains lossy.
- **Explicit capability and geometry checks.** The host probes each codec and bit depth separately, keeps display dimensions separate from the packed coded dimensions, and rejects unsupported requests without substituting 4:2:0 or software encoding. Unsupported narrow HEVC surfaces are rejected before AMF initialization.
- **Keep the 200 Mbps transport budget.** Nightjar reserves space for packet headers, encryption and FEC, limiting packed video payload to 140 Mbps at a configured 200 Mbps total budget.

**Verification and limits:** Exact packing tests, independent FFmpeg decoding, protocol checks, Windows reconstruction tests, Linux desktop checks and Android builds passed. On an AMD Radeon 780M, all four packed modes reached approximately 1440p120 in bounded synthetic tests (10 measured seconds per mode): calculated peak transport bandwidth including 20% FEC and packet headers was at most 192.8 Mbps, with at most 3.58 ms added host p95 latency versus matched 4:2:0. This does not establish sustained end-to-end performance. Physical HDR presentation on Android/Linux and Windows HDR output remain unverified. An earlier workstation crash remains unexplained; the later bounded tests completed without a crash. Keep the feature experimental and disabled on unverified devices.

## New in 2.5.11

- **Return completed capture buffers without a cursor update.** Since 2.5.6, completed GPU conversions could keep all four frame-push capture slots owned when no separate cursor overlay was drawn. PyroWave then waited for a new frame that capture could not produce. Conversion now releases completed slots on every frame. This is unrelated to Nightjar's local cursor setting and addresses a reproduced cause of the slowdown investigated in [#42](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/42).
- **Keep PyroWave alignment inside the wire budget.** Record padding could enlarge a frame after the codec had applied its bitrate budget: a native GPU fixture configured for 400 Mbps produced packets requiring about 447 Mbps at 60 fps. Alignment is retained when its packets, headers and adaptive critical parity fit; otherwise the existing unpadded record format preserves the encoded pixels. Recovery protection can be reduced on that fallback, with the existing warning retained. The budget also covers lower adaptive FEC percentages that restore parity at the 255-shard limit. The padding issue predates 2.5.6.
- **Interrupt packet pacing when a PyroWave stream stops.** An extremely low pacing cap could leave disconnect waiting for a long packet deadline and send another packet afterward. Shutdown now wakes that wait and rechecks cancellation before sending, without changing normal pacing or its adaptive wake lead.

**Verification and limits:** All 1,302 workspace tests, six native AMD Radeon 780M GPU tests, 78 release-tooling tests, formatting and workspace Clippy passed. The capture regression fails before the fifth frame without its fix and completes 16 frames with it. The framing regression checks 12 scene/bitrate combinations at every adaptive critical FEC percentage from 0 to 20 and preserves decoded pixels. The real-sender cancellation regression fails before its fix and now completes teardown within 250 ms without another packet. Independent QA reviewed the changes. The desktop AMD driver reset remains unexplained, and performance recovery on the reporter's setup still needs a live stream test.

## New in 2.5.10

- **Use the virtual display again for clients that send `virtualDisplay=0`.** Since 2.5.6 the host read `virtualDisplay=0` as a request for the physical display. Clients built for Apollo send it whenever their own virtual display option is off, and many have no such option at all, so they streamed the PC's monitor at its resolution and bit depth instead of getting a virtual display at their own (a 2560×1600 client mirrored a 3440×1440 monitor, and 10-bit SDR became 8-bit). Apollo reads `0` as "not forced", and Rubylight now does too; the host, app and device settings decide. `hostDisplay=physical` still selects the physical display.
- **Keep the requested resolution when Windows refuses its refresh.** With VRR on, the host asks the virtual display for 1000 Hz. On a Windows 11 23H2 PC, Windows dropped 4K at 1000 Hz from the mode list. Up to 2.5.7 the driver's EDID declared 495 Hz and a 510 kHz line rate at most (2.5.8 removed those limits), and libvirtualdisplay, whose EDID declares 255 Hz at most, lost 1000 Hz on the same PC too. libvirtualdisplay only kept 4K because its fixed list also has 4K at 240 Hz. Rubylight's monitor offered no other 4K rate, so Windows fell back to 1440p or 1080p. The driver now also offers the requested resolution at the slower of 60, 120, 240 and 480 Hz and keeps it as the preferred timing, and the host retries a refused mode at the fastest rate Windows accepts at that resolution. A 138 fps VRR stream then gets 4K at 480 or 240 Hz instead of a lower resolution. Reported in [#40](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/40).
- **Stop saving layouts that Windows chose for a device.** An "Extended + primary" stream whose display never became primary saved "Extended" for the device at disconnect, and that device layout then overrode every later change to the host layout, Exclusive included. A layout is now saved only when the planned one took effect at the start of the stream; one that did not is reported as a warning. A saved layout gives way once the app or host layout it replaced changes, and a layout picked in the device editor stays. Reported in [#40](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/40). If a device still ignores the host layout after updating, set its layout back to the host setting under **Devices**.
- **Remember the resolution chosen in Windows.** Leave the device's display mode empty, turn on **Devices → (device) → Remember the resolution I pick**, and change the stream's virtual display resolution in Windows display settings. When the device's last stream disconnects, Rubylight keeps that resolution, puts it back if the stream resumes, and creates the display at it from the next stream. Only the resolution is kept: the refresh still follows the stream and the virtual display refresh setting, VRR included, and the stream keeps the size and frame rate the client asks for. Requested in [#36](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/36).
- **Stream in the background while someone uses the PC.** A device or app can stream on an isolated virtual display that never shows the PC's monitor. Game windows move there without taking focus, games keep their sound and frame rate, audio carries only the game, input is limited to controllers, the clipboard is off, and Windows background throttling is turned off for the game. Requested in [#43](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/43).
- **Answer clock sync probes.** Clients can map frame timestamps to their own clock through control message 0x5537, for VRR timestamp presentation and end-to-end latency measurement.
- **Run the PyroWave link probe at streaming priority.** Since 2.5.9's timer waits give the CPU up between packets, a probe beside busy threads could wake late for every batch and report a far slower link than the stream gets. The probe now runs at the priority of the stream's senders.

All workspace tests and Clippy pass. The display fixes are covered by unit tests; none has been streamed on the reporters' setups yet.

## New in 2.5.9

- **Restore PyroWave frame rate while a game is running.** Since 2.5.6, a PyroWave stream at 2772×1280, 144 fps and 400 Mbps on an RX 6600 XT peaked near 58 fps and averaged 35–55, where 2.5.1 averaged 78–118 and reached 129–144 with the same game and client. Picture age p95 rose from 1–2 ms to 6–11 ms. 2.5.6 had stopped raising the host and its capture helper to high CPU priority. Beside a busy game, the host's sending threads woke late, and the packet pacer lost each delay beyond its 0.5 ms slack. PyroWave keeps a frame on the wire for most of its period, so these delays cut its frame rate directly. High priority is back while streaming. Packet pacing now also wakes early by the lateness it has measured, instead of yielding through the last 600 µs as 2.5.1 did, so it does not occupy a CPU core. Reported in [#42](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/42).

A new socket-free probe (`examples/pace_probe.rs`) sends 144 fps PyroWave-sized frames at 500 Mbps beside one spinning thread per logical CPU. The new wait held 144 fps with a p95 wire time of 3.6 ms in every run. The 2.5.1 yielding wait reached only 104–136 fps, and the plain 2.5.6 timer dropped to 133 fps in one normal-priority run. All workspace tests, Clippy and the elevated process-priority restoration test pass. The comparison above comes from the reporter's 2.5.1 and 2.5.7 support logs. 2.5.9 has not yet been streamed on that setup.

## New in 2.5.8

- **Correct high-refresh virtual-display metadata.** The driver requested 1000 Hz for VRR while its EDID declared a 495 Hz maximum and clock limits too low for 4K1000. The updated driver removes those contradictory limits and lets IddCx describe its supported modes. The requested refresh, HDR metadata and EDID checksums are preserved.

This corrects a driver defect found while investigating [#40](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/40). Resolution of the reporter's Windows 11 23H2 rejection remains unconfirmed. The new [display trace helper](tests/trace_display_modes.ps1) captures Windows' rejection details without changing display, driver or service settings.

## New in 2.5.7

- **Restore Vulkan HDR layer loading on Windows.** The registered manifest path mixed backslashes and a forward slash, which made the Windows Vulkan loader look for the layer DLL in the installation folder instead of its `vulkan-layer` subfolder. Vulkan games could therefore miss HDR even with the setting enabled and a working HDR stream. Startup now registers the native Windows path and removes this installation's older, broken registration; disabling the setting also removes the broken enabled entry. Other installations and third-party layers are preserved. This addresses [Baldur's Gate 3 HDR detection (#39)](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/39). After updating, fully exit and relaunch the game while connected to an HDR stream.

Validated with isolated Windows registry regression tests and the real Windows Vulkan loader: the old registration fails to load the DLL, the corrected registration loads it, and inactive surface-format queries retain their original SDR results. Baldur's Gate 3 itself has not been tested locally.

- **Set a global default for closing apps on disconnect.** Settings → Library can close the app when its last streaming device leaves. Apps can use the global default or explicitly choose On or Off; existing app choices are preserved. The default stays off and changes apply on the next app launch. Addresses [#38](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/38).

## New in 2.5.6

**This release comes out of a dedicated bug hunt, and updating is advised.** We went looking for the causes of the recent resets, frozen streams, display mix-ups and input glitches, and fixed everything we could confirm, from display restoration and Windows input through GPU synchronization, PyroWave recovery, pacing and scheduling. If you have run into any of these, this is the build to install. We hope it is now the stable Rubylight you have been waiting for.

**Tested.** All 1,275 workspace tests and clippy pass on this build. We also streamed it on an RX 7900 XT (Windows 26200) to a laptop client: 2880×1800 at 165 Hz, AV1 10-bit HDR, frame-push capture from the Rubylight display, 88 Mbps configured. Over about four and a half minutes of desktop use, measured in 51 five-second samples:

| Measurement | Mean | Worst sample |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Host time per frame | 0.71 ms | p99 2.10 ms, max 2.76 ms |
| AMF encode | 3.64 ms | p99 5.03 ms |
| Present to sent | 4.00 ms | p99 9.08 ms |

No frame was lost and no reference was invalidated; the only keyframes were the two at stream start. Frame rate followed screen activity, reaching 165 fps whenever the desktop moved. That link lost nothing, so the new loss recovery, device memory and FEC hold below are covered by tests only so far, not by a lossy stream.

- **Size AMD recovery frames to the current packet pace.** Automatic frame caps allow 2–8 nominal frame budgets, with about two frame periods of wire time after parity/header allowance. Caps follow pace, bitrate and FPS before input submission. Explicit numeric caps, including driver default `0`, retain their meaning. Recovery logs report the cap associated with each picture and its packetized wire budget.
- **Remember devices that lost video frames.** Eligible reconnects at the same authenticated device/address start at the previously selected Wi-Fi pace for up to 24 hours, with a fast-rate probe every fifth eligible session. Devices includes a reset control. Manual pacing and either adaptive controller keep their own policies.
- **Retain error correction longer after loss.** Adaptive FEC holds for 30 seconds and then decays one percentage point every five seconds. Recovery frames still receive the configured ceiling. HEVC long-term references, AV1 confirmation keyframes and the isolated-loss threshold are unchanged.
- **Restore local displays after disconnect.** Display restoration now defaults on with a three-second delay. Exclusive layouts always release after the configured delay, including older profiles that disabled restoration. The final stream restores monitors its layout disabled even after Win+P changes; failed restoration keeps its original recovery baseline. Explicit `virtualDisplay=0` selects a physical display and cannot reuse a retained virtual stream.
- **Keep the virtual-display adapter through sleep.** Power transitions preserve the IddCx adapter and monitor identities while capture resources are stopped before sleep and recreated after wake. A confirmed failed Rubylight driver can be restarted by a session operation with bounded retries; healthy and manually disabled devices are left alone.
- **Use high GPU priority on Radeon and reject retired compute queues.** The host no longer requests process-wide realtime GPU priority on non-NVIDIA adapters. Converters, cursor preparation and capture handoff reject retired queues before submitting further work. The cause of the reported long-session AMD reset remains unconfirmed.
- **Reduce streaming CPU contention.** Packet and frame waits use high-resolution timers throughout, with frame waits remaining interruptible. The host and capture helper no longer raise their entire processes to high CPU priority. Notification-capable capture sources wait longer after the desktop becomes static, while active capture and pending GPU transfers retain short polls. Control-session and gamepad batches reuse their allocated buffers.
- **Keep paused layout changes and failed input out of later sessions.** Device layout preferences are captured when the last stream disconnects, so later local Win+P changes are not saved as streaming defaults. Touch state advances only after Windows accepts it, unknown-contact moves are ignored, and failed cancellation gets bounded cleanup before the synthetic device is retired. Final mouse/key releases are retried once without releasing another client's held controls.
- **Reject unsupported encoder resource states.** Compute conversion checks AMF's recorded texture state before recording a barrier. Video-only, graphics-only and invalid combined states return an explicit error rather than reaching the compute queue; no such state was observed in the reported reset.
- **Respect PyroWave path limits and loss feedback.** Unmeasured paths start near their wire budget; calibrated client limits and explicit pacing caps are never raised to fit an oversized stream. PyroWave now participates in the loss guard, opt-in pacing/bitrate adaptation and adaptive FEC. Legacy clients gain standard parity within their packet limits, with warnings if protection is reduced. Calibration uses the same batched sender as streaming, and legacy clients no longer accumulate larger frames after pauses.
- **Stop failed PyroWave GPU work before exposing output.** Vulkan completion is checked before reading a frame. Failed submissions no longer leave an unsignalled wait on capture's D3D11 context, and resources still potentially used by the GPU remain owned. An unresolved failure disables further PyroWave initialization on that adapter until the host restarts.
- **Correct SDR BT.2020 and standard-video chroma placement.** GPU and CPU conversion apply the SDR gamut and transfer conversion. Standard 4:2:0 conversion samples chroma at its signalled left position; PyroWave retains its specified centred position.
- **Keep D3D12 recovery ownership tied to the correct fence.** Retired queues cannot lend their fence values to replacements. Unfinished teardown retains GPU resources, and an unresolved retained queue prevents repeated allocations on the same adapter. Converter setup checks planar write support, released targets receive bounded completion waits, and AMF capacity waits avoid continuous CPU spinning.
- **Reuse recurring cursor shapes.** Bounded caches retain immutable D3D11 and D3D12 cursor textures across animation frames; queued work keeps its resources even after cache eviction.

- **Keep shared textures owned after failed GPU ordering.** A failed signal or wait now retires the compute queue before a keyed-mutex texture can be returned to its producer. Capture must reopen before that queue can lend or return more textures.
- **Track recovery keyframes by their matching AMF submission.** If the driver omits its output-type property, buffered startup and recovery outputs use their effective keyframe request. IDRs forced by rejected long-term-reference properties now reach submission tracking and acknowledgements too.
- **Transition uploaded cursor textures before compute sampling.** New shapes now enter the shader-read state after upload. The native regression failed for both shapes before correction and passed afterward; HEVC/AV1 SDR/HDR checks with cursor changes produced zero Direct3D errors and independently decoded all 128 frames.

See the [audit, verification and unresolved reset](../docs/stability-audit-2026-10-18.md). An additional reset on installed 2.5.5 used standard keyed-mutex capture and required a Windows restart. These corrections do not establish the cause of that reset or long-session stability.

The [PyroWave and D3D12 follow-up](../docs/pyrowave-d3d12-audit-2026-10-18.md) records the later corrections, compatibility tradeoffs and verification limits.

## New in 2.5.5

- **Correct recycled AMF D3D12 input barriers.** The converter now reads the resource state AMF records after encoding before transitioning a reused texture for conversion. AMF left both HEVC and AV1 inputs in `COPY_SOURCE`; assuming `COMMON` caused invalid barriers on both YUV planes. A short native reproduction reported 124 Direct3D validation errors before the fix and zero afterward. HEVC and AV1 SDR/HDR checks independently decoded all 128 frames. This corrects a GPU synchronization defect; longer use is still needed to determine its contribution to the reported AMD resets. See the [investigation and validation](PERFORMANCE_WORK.md#2026-10-17-amf-d3d12-input-state-reuse).

## New in 2.5.4

- **Improve HEVC intra-refresh compatibility.** Clients that negotiate HEVC intra-refresh now use a finite GOP and refresh whole CTB rows, following Eftimin70's Xbox workaround and Nonary/vibeshine's follow-up. Both settings are checked before and after encoder initialization. Drivers that reject them retain the previous policy; a failed candidate initialization gets one fresh attempt using that policy. Startup and recovery keyframes remain enabled.
- **Respect local display changes while a game is disconnected.** A paused game keeps its virtual-display lease without switching the display back on after a local Win+P change or restoring its mode/HDR. Resuming reactivates the display and publishes the capture target before streaming starts. Active streams retain display recovery. This addresses a code path relevant to [issue #35](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/35); the full reported freeze and touchscreen symptom remain unconfirmed.
- **Restore 1440p virtual-display modes.** The updated driver includes 2560x1440 at 60, 120 and 240 Hz even for a 4K stream. Combining mode lists also keeps custom refresh rates and the preferred refresh order. Run this release's installer to update the display driver.

**Validation and limits:** 574 host/Windows automated tests, formatting and Clippy passed for the display change. The HEVC candidate independently decoded all 1,800 SDR/HDR frames and both 167-frame recovery tails on an RX 7900 XT at 4K119.88. A user who could reproduce the colour smearing confirmed it was gone on Xbox Series S with the latest Xbox Moonlight client, using the 2.5.3 candidate at 4K119.88 HEVC HDR; its HEVC code is unchanged in 2.5.4. The native paused-display test could not run because Windows denied driver access. These results do not establish a general picture-quality or latency gain. The separate HDR-toggle report in [issue #36](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/36) remains unresolved. See the [HEVC test method and results](PERFORMANCE_WORK.md#2026-10-17-negotiated-hevc-intra-refresh-candidate).

In 2.5.4, restoring the physical monitor layout automatically after Moonlight disconnects requires enabling **Settings → Display → Restore displays on disconnect**. That release defaults to off with a three-second restore delay; the unreleased changes above enable restoration by default.

## New in 2.5.3

- **Restore 1440p virtual-display modes.** The display driver includes 2560x1440 at 60, 120 and 240 Hz even when the stream requests 4K. Combining mode lists also preserves custom refresh rates and the preferred refresh order. Install this release's updated display driver through the installer to receive the fix.
- **Apply the negotiated HEVC intra-refresh workaround.** Clients that request HEVC intra-refresh now use a finite GOP and refresh whole CTB rows, following Eftimin70's Xbox workaround and Nonary/vibeshine's follow-up. At 4K this requests GOP 120 and 60 CTBs per slot. Both settings are verified before and after encoder initialization; rejected settings restore the previous policy, and candidate initialization failures get one fresh encoder attempt using that policy. Startup and recovery keyframes remain enabled.

**Validation and limits:** 524 core/AMF unit tests and Clippy passed. On an RX 7900 XT at 4K119.88, independent decoding passed all 1,800 SDR/HDR frames and both 167-frame recovery tails. No periodic keyframe bursts or packet-budget violations appeared in those synthetic GPU tests. These checks do not establish a picture-quality or latency gain or confirm the workaround on Xbox Series X / RX 9070 XT. The separate HDR-toggle report in [issue #36](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/36) remains unresolved. See the [test method and results](PERFORMANCE_WORK.md#2026-10-17-negotiated-hevc-intra-refresh-candidate).

## New in 2.5.2

- **Retry brief Windows send-buffer exhaustion.** Error `10055` now gets one short backoff and retry for video batches and audio datagrams, within the existing shared four-millisecond wait budget. Persistent failures still drop promptly; successful sends are never repeated.
- **React to repeated unrecoverable video frames.** The default loss guard also reduces packet bursts after three distinct recent frames arrive incomplete within two seconds. Repeated block reports count once per frame. Encoder bitrate stays unchanged, and manual pacing or either adaptive controller retains control.
- **Hand off as soon as loss feedback is available.** Loss feedback can move the next output directly to the video sender thread, avoiding an extra paced frame on the capture thread. Existing queues and frame ordering are preserved.
- **Recover the final driver-pushed picture after temporary pressure.** When every shared frame slot is busy, the display driver retains the newest image and retries publication once a slot becomes available. Fresh acquisitions take precedence, retries preserve the original timestamps, and a successful image is not repeatedly published. Capture consumers are also notified before the previous image is released.
- **Trace fresh-picture delivery when diagnosing low FPS.** Optional capture, admission and packetizer traces can distinguish newly acquired frames, repeated source pictures and time spent waiting. They are off by default.

See the [native loss-response checks and remaining AMF frame-size limitation](PERFORMANCE.md#october-17-loss-response-verification).

**Validation and limits:** 1,189 workspace tests passed before the version bump; short local stream checks decoded all 1,739 received frames, and the matched UDP checks lost no packets. These checks do not establish a general network-throughput or latency gain. The retained-picture driver policy has regression coverage; its new driver was not installed for those stream measurements. AMD can still exceed an accepted frame-size limit, including in the reported RX 9070 XT case. Encoder defaults and the experimental fence option remain unchanged.

## New in 2.5.1

- **Safer recovery from GPU fence failures.** Failed synchronization retires the affected compute queue and reopens capture with the standard keyed-mutex transport. The same display registration remembers the failure through capture restarts; recreating the display permits a fresh attempt. An active producer that cannot publish its first frame falls back sooner, while a display that has not begun producing pixels keeps its startup allowance.
- **Bounded producer wait for CPU readback.** CPU readers wait at most two seconds for a captured frame's producer fence before submitting the staging copy. A missing completion returns an error and rejects the affected fence ring so capture can recover. GPU conversion keeps its asynchronous waits; this deadline does not cover unrelated later copy, mapping or driver stalls.

GPU fence transport remains **experimental and off by default**. Installed-driver comparisons found shorter host-side capture steps, but variable picture age and repeated source pictures under graphics load do not support making it automatic. Existing capture choices and the standard keyed-mutex transport remain available. See the [26,592-frame comparison, timing definitions and limits](PERFORMANCE.md#october-17-installed-driver-fence-comparison).

## New in 2.5.0

- **Smoother AMD recovery after a keyframe.** Proven depth-one AMF pipelines can prepare one fresh picture near the end of a paced recovery frame. Four short RX 7900 XT comparisons reduced the gap from an IDR to its next fresh received frame from **8.44 to 5.30 ms**, with **6,941/6,941 frames decoded**. Queue bounds, packet order, FEC, network pacing and the Punktfunk-compatible Moonlight host metric remain unchanged. Unknown/deeper encoder pipelines retain their required priming; socket stalls and drops restore the strict barrier. This improves the measured recovery cadence; overall picture age did not consistently improve.
- **Antialiased GPU downscaling.** Fractional and larger reductions average the source pixels each output pixel covers, reducing thin-line shimmer and lost detail when streaming below the desktop resolution. Native resolution, enlargement and aligned 2× reduction preserve their existing pixels. Work is bounded to reductions up to 8× per axis; larger reductions retain the previous filter. Existing HDR transfer handling, cursor composition and aspect-ratio behavior are preserved. An independent reference checks pixels across D3D11 graphics, D3D11 compute and D3D12 compute paths; separate timing and encoded-output checks document the cost.
- **An additional experimental GPU fence transport.** Advanced Video settings can enable `idd_push_fence_ring` for direct driver push on compatible AMD setups. It adds D3D12-owned shared textures and GPU fences to the existing D3D11 display driver. Every existing capture method and encoder remains available, and unsupported drivers or failed setup use the standard transport. The option is **off by default**: component tests reduced CPU handoff overhead, but completed-conversion gains were small and variable. Installed-driver end-to-end gains and native D3D12 IddCx swap-chain support remain unverified.

See the [2.5.0 measurements, settings and limits](PERFORMANCE.md#october-17-focused-250-comparisons). These focused RX 7900 XT comparisons do not establish a market-wide ranking or an input-to-display latency result.

## New in 2.4.8

- **Moonlight host timing matches Punktfunk's GameStream software path.** The timer starts at the capture/encode output tick and stops at entry into packetization. Conventional video and PyroWave prepare packets before handing them to a separate paced sender, so the prepared packets' queue and send time cannot inflate the value. Packetizer backlog is still counted. Asynchronous output uses the tick that polls it; the original encoder duration and full present-to-sent timing remain separate measurements. The wire field truncates and saturates exactly as Punktfunk's does.
- **Send delays can be diagnosed from ordinary logs.** Conventional output records its prepared-packet queue separately and logs unusually slow frames with their size, effective pacing rate, expected wire time, pacing waits and socket waits. Network pacing limits are unchanged.
- **Smaller AMD recovery frames by default.** Native AMD AMF encoders now request a maximum frame size of two nominal frame budgets (`amd_max_frame_size=2`). In an isolated RX 7900 XT HEVC test at 4K119.88 and 70.77 Mbps pacing, recovery-frame pacing fell from 28–54 ms to about 15 ms; all 7,112 received frames decoded. Unsupported or unapplied limits at encoder setup warn and keep streaming with the driver's effective setting. The driver may exceed an accepted limit, and limiting large frames can reduce picture quality during recovery or scene changes. Choose **Maximum frame size → Driver default** (`0`) to restore unrestricted frame sizes. See the [measured settings and limitations](PERFORMANCE.md#existing-amf-frame-size-limit-at-the-observed-pace).

## New in 2.4.7

> [!IMPORTANT]
> **Still using WGC on a Rubylight virtual display?** Choose **Settings → Video → Capture method → Direct driver push** to use the recommended, lower-overhead path straight from Rubylight's display driver. **Automatic already uses this path** on Rubylight virtual displays. Save, end existing streams and reconnect. Physical monitors still use Windows capture; lower capture overhead does not guarantee lower total latency in every setup.

- **Select Direct driver push in Video settings.** The Capture method menu now exposes Rubylight's existing direct virtual-display capture path. Automatic's help explains that it already uses driver push on Rubylight virtual displays and WGC elsewhere. The console identifies the selected method and keeps capture-specific controls relevant. Driver push falls back to WGC when unavailable; existing capture selections are preserved.
- **Create a token with all available API permissions.** Token creation now offers **Select all permissions**, including paths hidden by search, while keeping individual selection and clearing available. The old 64-path limit blocked tokens selecting the host's full catalog; the UI and host now accept all advertised paths and methods. Token-management routes remain administrator-only.

## New in 2.4.6

- **Your phone's microphone becomes the PC's microphone automatically.** When microphone forwarding starts, Rubylight selects Steam Streaming Microphone for all Windows recording roles, so apps using the system default can hear the client. Muting or disconnecting restores the previous microphones; manual selections during forwarding are preserved. Recovery also covers host crashes and failed switches. Apps pinned to a particular input still need **Default** selected, and an already-open voice session may need restarting. If Windows refuses the automatic switch, forwarding stays available with a message explaining manual input selection.

## New in 2.4.5

- **Nonary's Moonlight can calibrate PyroWave against Rubylight.** Its calibration measures the fastest total rate, and the fastest frame pace, your network carries without loss, using the UDP probe added in 2.4.4. It needs the host to treat that rate as a total on the wire, as Vibeshine does, which Rubylight now does. A PyroWave bitrate is now the stream's whole budget: audio with its parity and 500 kbps for control come off it. With record framing (Nonary's Moonlight, Nightjar, Aurora), each picture gets what fits after packet overhead and one block of critical FEC. **The picture gets about 15–20% less than the bitrate you set**, and the stream no longer goes over that bitrate on the wire. Raise the client's bitrate if your network has room.

## New in 2.4.4

- **The console's certificate warning is gone after an update too.** 2.4.2 and 2.4.3 trusted the console's root only on a fresh install, so a PC updated from an earlier version still showed the browser warning on `https://localhost:47990`. Setup now trusts it on every install and update.
- **PyroWave clients can measure their own network.** Paired clients can ask the host for a two-second UDP test at a chosen rate, optionally sent as whole frames at a chosen pace, and count what arrives. This is the same `/pyrowave-udp-probe` that Vibeshine serves, including its handshake for client firewalls. A client can use it to find the fastest pace its network carries without loss and report it with each stream, so a Wi-Fi client is no longer sent frames faster than it can take them. The probe is refused while a stream runs.

## New in 2.4.3

- **Steadier frame timing when a game runs between the stream's rate and the display's.** On Rubylight's 240 Hz virtual display, a game whose pictures land on one refresh and then two (about 160 a second) could make the host encode 121 frames a second for a 120 fps stream, taking pictures that had already waited. Rubylight now judges the source by its average rate and stays at the stream's rate.
- **Streams start when the GPU driver refuses the client's slice count ([#32](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/32)).** Moonlight for Nintendo Switch asks for four slices per frame. When the AMD driver rejected that count, the encoder failed and the stream never started. Rubylight still asks for the client's count, but now logs a warning and keeps the driver's own value. A tile count set in `amd_av1_tiles` is still required.

## New in 2.4.2

- **No more certificate warning on the console.** Opening `https://localhost:47990` on the host PC showed Firefox's and other browsers' security warning, because the console reused the self-signed certificate that paired devices check. The console now has its own certificate for `localhost`, `127.0.0.1` and `::1`, and setup adds its issuer, **Rubylight local console**, to Windows' trusted roots. That root can vouch only for those loopback names, and its private key is deleted once it has signed the console's certificate, so trusting it cannot vouch for any other site. Firefox uses Windows' trusted roots by default. Paired devices are unaffected. Opened from another device by the host's network address, the console still shows the warning.

## New in 2.4.1

- **Your display layout is remembered ([#33](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/33)).** Switching Windows to **Show only on 2** (only the stream's display), or making the stream's display the main one, during an extended stream was undone at the next connection. Rubylight now keeps the layout you leave a stream in as that device's own, shown as its **Virtual display layout** under **Devices**. A layout your client asks for, and the exclusive and isolated layouts, are left as they are.
- **PyroWave on a client with a slower network port ([#31](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/31)).** PyroWave sent each frame at the host's own link speed, so a 10 GbE host streaming to a 2.5 GbE client, or a 2.5 GbE host to a 1 GbE client, overran the client's port: shimmering and lost packets unless the bitrate went far down. Clients that report their own network link with each PyroWave stream, as Nonary's Moonlight does, now get frames paced to that link when it is slower than the host's, or to the pace their calibration found. A client as fast as the host keeps the full speed, so its frames arrive just as soon. `pacing_max_bitrate_kbps` still sets a fixed limit.
- **Steadier frame timing on Rubylight's virtual displays.** A pacing wait meant for Windows Graphics Capture also ran when frames came from Rubylight's display driver; under GPU load it could hold a frame up to a quarter of a frame period past its slot. It now runs only with Windows Graphics Capture.

## New in 2.4.0

- **Rubylight's own virtual display driver.** Setup now installs Rubylight's display driver instead of libvirtualdisplay (SudoVDA), and removes libvirtualdisplay from earlier installations once Rubylight's driver runs. The host reads each frame straight from the driver, which saves about 0.9 ms at 240 Hz over Windows Graphics Capture. Until releases are signed, setup signs the driver on your PC with a key it deletes at once; no test mode, no restart. On PCs with Smart App Control, Windows in S mode or an App Control policy, Windows refuses such a driver: setup and the console say so and what you can do, nothing is left half-installed, and streams use a physical display. Two devices streaming one virtual display with different settings both get a picture: the second captures it with Windows Graphics Capture.
- **NVIDIA RTX HDR (TrueHDR) is gone.** It was the one part of Rubylight that linked NVIDIA's proprietary libraries, and it only ran on NVIDIA GPUs; without it every Rubylight build is open source, which code signing through the SignPath Foundation requires. HDR streams of HDR games and displays are unchanged. The peak brightness a virtual display reports stays, as **Settings → HDR → Peak brightness** (`hdr_peak_brightness`, earlier `rtx_hdr_peak_brightness`, which is read under its new name). Other saved RTX HDR settings are dropped without a warning.
- **Version details on every Rubylight program.** The host, service, start program, setup, diagnostic probes and Rubylight's DLLs now carry the product name, version and a description, which Explorer's **Details** tab and Task Manager show. Code signing through the SignPath Foundation requires them.
- **Code signing policy and privacy statement.** The [code signing policy](../docs/code-signing-policy.md) says who signs releases and how to check a download; the [privacy statement](../docs/privacy.md) lists every connection Rubylight makes and how to turn it off. Releases stay unsigned until the SignPath project is approved.
- **See how old a picture is when it leaves the host.** Moonlight's host latency starts when the host claims a picture for encoding and stops before it is sent, so it leaves out the capture and the send. The stream card now also shows **Present to sent**: the average from Windows' present to the last packet sent, for new pictures, with its 99th percentile. The 5-second `stream timings` log line adds where that time goes, stage by stage (`present_to_sent_ms`, `stage_*_ms`). Moonlight's own figure is unchanged. [What each host's figure covers](../docs/performance.md#comparing-host-latency-between-hosts)
- **Smoother streams once pacing slows down.** On a fast wired link the host sends each frame itself between pictures. When pacing later slowed, for example after a lost keyframe, every send took up to half a frame period and held up the next picture. Those sends now move to the video send thread, as on a link paced from the start. Under congestion, a batch of packets now waits at most 4 ms in total for a full socket buffer instead of 4 ms per packet, which could hold a frame for a quarter of a second without network offload. Reading the stream's statistics, for the console or the 5-second log, no longer holds up the send thread while it sorts them.

## New in 2.3.1

Frame rate and slow-network fixes, measured on an RX 7900 XT host: Warp-style requests now get the frame rate they ask for, and TV boxes with 100 Mbps Ethernet get a picture.

- **A picture on TV boxes with 100 Mbps Ethernet.** Behind a port slower than the host and a switch with a shallow queue, every keyframe sent at the fast wired pacing rate (up to 800 Mbps) overflowed the queue. The client asked for another, which was lost the same way, and the picture froze. Through a 100 Mbps loopback bottleneck with a 16 or 64 KiB queue, 1440p60 AV1 and HEVC streams at 40 and 60 Mbps delivered no frame in 20 seconds, in 2.2.1 too. Now, once a keyframe is lost, the rest of the stream is paced like Wi-Fi (twice the encoder bitrate), and the picture arrived within about a tenth of a second. Devices that never lose a keyframe keep the fast rate. **Settings → Video → Slow down after lost keyframes** (`keyframe_loss_pacing`) turns it off.
- **Streams run at the frame rate the device asks for.** Artemis's Warp frame pacing launches at 60 fps and asks for 120 or 240 in its stream request; the host streamed 60, so the frame rate would not go above 60 whatever Warp setting was chosen. The stream now runs at the requested rate, as in Vibepollo: a virtual display's refresh is raised for it and the frame limiter follows it. The bitrate you set stays the stream's budget, so each frame gets less of it than in Vibepollo at the same setting; raise the bitrate if your network allows. **Use the launch frame rate** (`limit_framerate`), now off by default, keeps the launch rate.
- **The console shows the frame rate the stream is encoded at.** With **Use the launch frame rate** on, a device that launches at 60 fps and asks for 120 in its stream request is streamed at 60 fps, but the stream card showed 120 as the target. It now shows 60 and the rate the device asked for.
- **Send Artemis's Warp streams at the bitrate you set.** With Artemis's Frame pacing set to Warp Drive or Warp 2, the host multiplied the client's bitrate by 2 or 4 but still encoded at the launch frame rate, so a 40 Mbps setting sent about 84 or 168 Mbps. On a TV box with a 100 Mbps Ethernet port, such as the Onn 4K Pro, nearly every frame was lost. The bitrate you set is now the stream's budget whatever the frame pacing. The log's stream lines also show the requested frame rate, the configured and encoder bitrates and the rate actually sent.

Measured with isolated test hosts and the independent moonlight-common-c receiver: a request launched at 60 fps that asks for 120 delivered 120.0 fps on a virtual display (2.3.0: 60.0), and 120 → 240 delivered 240.0 fps; the virtual display's refresh was raised in place and the bitrate stayed at the client's setting. Through a 100 Mbps loopback bottleneck with a 16 or 64 KiB queue, every frame of 1440p60 AV1 and HEVC arrived (2.3.0 and 2.2.1: none). A real Onn 4K Pro and Artemis have not been tested.

## New in 2.3.0

A fresh start. The 2.2.x line shipped 21 updates in four days. 2.3.0 replaces them with one reviewed release: every change since 2.2.1 was audited, the regressions found are fixed with regression tests, and the web console was reworked. Upgrading from any 2.x keeps settings, paired devices and the app library.

- **A clearer web console.** Settings now uses one row layout: the setting on the left, its control on the right. Each description is one or two sentences, with details behind **More**. The default and **Reset** appear only when a value differs, and config keys stay hidden unless **Show config keys** is on. Discarding unsaved changes asks first. Overview, Devices and Maintenance are reorganised: readiness checks are easy to scan, pairing uses PIN and one-time-PIN tabs, and Maintenance is grouped into Host, Displays, Troubleshooting, Integrations and Security. Every console request now has a time limit, so a host that stops answering can no longer leave buttons locked.
- **Pair devices with ordinary punctuation in their names.** Since 2.2.2, pairing refused names outside letters, digits and `- _ . '`. Android clients that send their model, such as "moto g(60)", could not pair, and names like "Galaxy S24+" or "Ramazan’s iPhone" were refused in the console.
- **Hybrid GPUs ([#28](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/28)).** With a GPU selected under Graphics card, Rubylight now asks the virtual display driver to render on that GPU, as Apollo and Vibepollo do. If the display still ends up on another GPU and Windows Graphics Capture is unavailable, Desktop Duplication captures and encodes on the GPU that renders it. Previously the stream ended without a picture. The stream card says which GPU is used.
- **Connect right after the host starts.** Since 2.2.3, a client that connected while the startup encoder check was running cached H.264/SDR, or its first launch was refused. Discovery and launches now wait up to five seconds for that first check, and never for retried checks.
- **Updates work again for installs outside Program Files.** Since 2.2.2, every update failed when the install folder, or a folder above it, belonged to a user, for example `D:\Rubylight` created in Explorer. Updating now takes ownership of such a folder and locks it down. When a folder above it belongs to a user, the installer offers to move Rubylight to `C:\Program Files\Rubylight`, keeping settings, paired devices and drivers. **Maintenance → Updates** now shows why an update stopped instead of "See the setup log".
- **Portable hosts started as administrator run apps again.** Since 2.2.2, every non-elevated app, preparation command and client hook failed to start with "bad impersonation level". They now start with the desktop's standard user rights.
- **Controllers follow the device that plays.** Since 2.2.16, switching devices during a game left the previous device's virtual controllers plugged in, where they could hold player 1.
- **Capture survives display changes during its colour check.** Since 2.2.17, a display change during the HDR/colour refresh restarted capture, or prevented it from opening.
- **Settings the console did not show.** Settings saved by Vibepollo, Apollo or 2.0.x under older names (`rtss_path`, `rtss_disable_vsync_ullm`, `nvenc_force_split_encode`, `dd_vdd_static_monitor_count`) now appear in the console under their current names. Values the host accepts in other spellings show as their option. Quoted numbers such as `"60"` are read as numbers.
- **Settings checks.** A fixed refresh rate with more than three decimals, or a malformed fixed resolution, is refused when saving instead of failing every stream. AMD encoder (AMF) settings are visible when the encoder is Automatic on an AMD GPU.
- **Apps on mapped drives and network shares start** under the service instead of reporting "executable not found".
- **Remote Desktop ([#18](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/18)).** A launch into a disconnected session is logged as a warning, with the session and its state. When a stream starts on the console sign-in screen while a signed-in Remote Desktop session is disconnected, the log explains that signing in reconnects that session and ends the stream. The console and troubleshooting guide describe this case.
- **The host's private key stays locked** when a profile permission reset fails during setup. Before, it could stay readable by every local user until the host restarted.

Validation: workspace tests (core, host, Windows, setup), strict Clippy, formatting, 42 console unit tests and 49 console browser tests. Hybrid-GPU rendering, a real install-folder move and the Remote Desktop cases need the affected hardware and are not yet confirmed there.

## New in 2.2.21

- **Keep the PC display primary when extending the desktop.** If Windows recalls a virtual display as primary when it reconnects, Extend and Isolate now restore the active display that was primary before the stream. Explicit virtual-primary and exclusive modes keep their existing behavior; disconnected displays are not reactivated.
- **Make display choices explicit.** Host settings, device overrides and app settings now say whether Extend or Isolate keeps the PC display primary or makes the virtual display primary. Stored choices and client/device/app override priority remain unchanged.

## New in 2.2.20

- **Show GPUs without an attached monitor in Graphics card ([#28](https://github.com/RamazanKara/Rubylight/issues/28)).** The list now includes all hardware GPUs reported by Windows DXGI, so a hybrid system's dedicated GPU is no longer omitted just because its displays connect to the integrated GPU. Automatic still uses the captured display's GPU; appearing in the list does not confirm encoder or codec support.

## New in 2.2.19

- **Offer adaptive packet pacing for difficult connections.** The existing fast route-based policy remains the default. Experimental adaptive pacing is an explicit option for H.264, HEVC and AV1: it starts conservatively, then probes faster rates with active video and healthy control feedback. Loss during a probe restores the preceding rate. Picture bitrate and FPS stay unchanged, but startup takes longer and probing can still lose packets. A suitable fixed pacing limit remains the simpler choice for a known constrained path. Explicit limits, PyroWave and opt-in host bitrate adaptation keep their own policies. The reported Onn issue is not confirmed resolved. [Configuration](../docs/configuration.md#capture-and-video).
